# Dedication

This book is dedicated to my father Joe, and my mother Joan, who live in the far less exciting world where you and I dwell.

# Introduction

MY NAME IS CHARLENE Schneider, but most people call me Charlie. I live in a castle on lake Geneva, but I am not a princess, and it is not the same Switzerland you know, nor the same World you live in. I am a negotiator, and what a negotiator is will become clear very quickly.

You might think this story is about me, but it isn't really. It's a story about great power and what people do with it, about a witch who was not a witch, and a dragon that was not a dragon. It is a story set between how much we know about the universe and how much we still have to learn.

Like all good stories, it is one that starts with a friendship and ends with a romance. It is however not a story for children, nor is it a story for the faint of heart.

# The Negotiator

MENDEZ SAT STONE-FACED and confident as both auditors left the room. The fake exchange security contracts for the two shell companies had been completely overlooked in the flood of paperwork that the purchasers and the independent auditors had requested.

So far the chief accountant had kept to the same story, one that would be very hard to disprove. The chief accountant of course, would not deviate one iota from this story; His house, his lifestyle, and his two kids' private school education deeply depended on the nine hundred thousand he had been fed over the past eighteen months. Jail time, and the crumbling of his family's high social standing would be the minimum he could expect if his involvement was ever found out.

The takeover of the bank had come at a perfect time for Mendez to sweep the last traces of the fraud under the carpet. He had used all his influence and a few costly favours to get appointed to the team that would assist the auditors. Now, as a fox in the hen-house, he was in a perfect position to be one step ahead of them. But it all depended upon getting through this audit, and it had stalled.

Once the buyout was completed, it would be easy to ‘lose’ the more incriminating paperwork in the transition and action the insolvency of the two companies. A few eyes might be raised at the cancellation of the large contracts, but there would be nothing they could do by then and the twenty seven million euros would be in his bank free and clear.

Let The ElderNacht think he was still supporting the stupid terrorists cause, he honestly could give a shit anymore. No, this last transaction would be all for him, and would be plenty to furnish the extravagant lifestyle he had already planned out in Honduras. They could keep their seventy-two virgins, he would make his own arrangement with god for a couple of hot Latino chicks, the boat and the beach villa.

He looked again at the other nineteen people around the massive boardroom table, each on a laptop, a tablet or head buried in some folder, he was the lowest of the low, yet he was running rings around all these so-called financial experts. He smiled at the boxes and boxes of folders piled high on every surface surrounding the room that nobody could ever realistically sort through, the real documents were not even here, they were faked and he was about to get away with it for the last time.

But the whole takeover was still awaiting the German audit team, the solicitor and the board of management from the purchasers signing off the accounts. Unfortunately the books didn't quite balance, and they never would.

Everybody on the seller's side were of course trying to be as helpful as possible, not because the sale to this new company was essential for future growth and prosperity, but for the large bonuses they would get from the inevitable share price increase.

The door to the large meeting room opened again and this time it was the Chief Financial Officer who walked in. How this man got the CFO position was something Mendez could not understand, the man’s appointment was nepotism at its finest; one where his golf handicap and who his brother was, was much more important than whatever his financial acumen was supposed to be.

The CFO was his usual inflated self, stout, well dressed and weasel looking. Not a hair from his coiffed head or manicured beard was out of place and his three thousand dollar suit hung just right around his suntanned torso. Just in case this was not enough to mark out his status, his gold Rolex and gold cufflinks were prominently displayed and sparkled for all to see.

The guy however was a joke and not fit for purpose. Mendez thought this company deserved to be taken for every penny it had if this was the high standard they adhered to.

The CFO’s golden smile was for once however hidden by a deep frown, and the reason for his frown became apparent as the CEO and solicitor for the purchasing company appeared behind him. The two company men sat down, but the purchaser’s solicitor remained standing. Something had happened.

“Due to the deadline for the completion of the negotiations being only a week away and the amount of work still to do by the auditors we find ourselves at an impasse. To resolve the impasse, a special negotiator has been requested and appointed.”

Several people turned and looked at each other, but the solicitor ignored them.

“Should this negotiator be satisfied with the conditions of the purchase and accept the current account portfolio then the purchasers will sign the agreement. If however there are any issues raised by the negotiator the takeover contract will be reviewed and may be suspended indefinitely.”

The solicitor delivered the monologue with deadly seriousness and there were a lot of worried faces looking at him, especially from the bank’s board of management.

Oh, this might work out just perfect, thought Mendez happily, his game face unchanged and rapt at attention like the remaining people around the table. Yes, just perfect!

“It is very unusual for an external negotiator to be called,” continued the solicitor. “But the purchaser's largest shareholder is a Swiss pension fund and, as doubts remain, they have insisted. All parties have therefore agreed to the appointment and conditions. She is en-route and will arrive at three.”

A negotiator, thought Mendez, what the hell is a negotiator? In what stupid system does one person, who knows nothing about the deal or the complexities, make a decision for a multi-billion dollar takeover? What the fuck was all that about!

“This is a very serious matter.” Came another voice, the one of the CEO who had now stood up and was sounding as serious as the solicitor.

“The appointment of the negotiator by us does not mean that she works for us. The negotiator is considered a neutral party, and even though the success of the purchase is our desired outcome, the purchasers will abide by whatever decision she arrives at.”

He shifted, looking at each of his team, and Mendez could see the same lingering doubts he had. The solicitor stood back up.

“I have been told that negotiators are also not known for hiding any illegal findings and wrong-doings, and by board agreement she is being given access to everything in your systems under a very strongly worded non-disclosure contract. Both the CEO and the information systems technical director will accompany her to ensure she has access to any system or document she needs.”

“The negotiator will also no doubt request several private one-to-one meetings with you or members of your staff, if this should happen then she will be accompanied by your human resources director.”

After a short silence, the CEO again spoke. “I ask each and everyone of you to speak openly and truthfully to her and go back to your staff and tell them the same. All of us want this acquisition to go through and none of us have anything to hide. I hope I have made myself clear.”

Mendez remained calm and content, but also confused as to what this negotiator was supposed to achieve in the four remaining days that teams of auditors working for the last five weeks could not. His mind went through all the permutations of the questions asked by the auditors and the documentation they demanded but thought of nothing. He glanced briefly at the chief accountant, detecting only the same slight nervousness as the rest of the people in the room.

“I have been informed that negotiators only work on facts, they will detect lies and rarely make wrong decisions, so whatever is currently going through your mind that a single person, with no relation to the company cannot possibly achieve what a large team of professionals can should be suspended.”

He paused, again looking at everyone and ensuring they understood.

“Are there any questions?”

Nobody spoke.

“If there are no further questions then this meeting is adjourned. The board will meet here at three which is the time she is due to arrive. Just be aware, there will be no notes, phones, laptops or recording devices permitted in this room that are not authorised by the negotiator, nobody will take notes but her, and nobody will discuss her, or what they discuss with her without her express permission. So please remove everything as you leave or the IT department who are coming in at 1 p.m. will remove it for you.”

The CEO got up and left, quickly followed by the solicitor and the purchasers. The people remaining looked at each other confused and dropped into small private group conversations before finally everyone including Mendez got up, gathered their laptops and tablets and left the room.

If the speech was supposed to intimidate Mendez, it failed!

* * *

Talk in the office the last twenty four hours had been reduced to whispers where the subject of the company acquisition, and the negotiator were concerned. Not just a woman apparently, but a young attractive one, almost a fucking girl!

Yet whoever she was, Mendez could see the effect she was having on the staff and department heads. From the moment she walked into the building his superiors had been running around in a panic as new requests for information came in.

The HR director was still in a state of shock.

When he first walked in the room and saw a young girl looking at him, his natural instinct to dominate began to rise. But before he had uttered a single word those dark eyes seemed to drill into his head.

“Sit.” Was all she said, firmly, assertively and with a tone nobody had used with him since sixth grade, a tone that said she did not just have the upper hand, but also the power of life or death over him. His almost primaeval response was to move back into a dominant position over the woman, but it was cut-off like a light switch as he saw the man with her, and without a word he sat down in the position indicated.

“Let us be clear Mr Weber, in this room I set the rules and you will not interfere or even talk unless I break a German employment law. I have no interest, nor do I care about the hundreds of silly laws your HR department has, I am talking about German Employment law itself. Therefore Mr Weber you will not speak because I know every single paragraph of those laws. Neither will you interrupt me, take notes if you must and follow up any queries after the interview, but do not do anything to alter the thread of my conversation.”

“If I say something it will be a fact not a guess. Should you interfere I will walk out of that door and explain to your board of management that I could not do my job because of you. They will then, as per contract, forfeit five million dollars and very probably the sale of your company. Do I make myself clear?”

“Perfectly clear,” he said after a few tense seconds.

“Good, then please watch, take notes, and maybe learn.”

He had indeed watched, took copious notes, and learned. He found himself both awed and very impressed.

For the next hour the negotiator called in each of the senior accounting staff and drilled deeply into details of their CV’s and work without references to documents or computers. She picked apart CV’s, asking technical terms he had never heard of and initiating three conference calls to cross-check details of previous companies the employees had supposedly worked at.

Within an hour, two employees CV’s were in tatters, indicating either bogus companies, bogus qualifications or bogus references. One of the men even broke down saying another member of staff had coerced him to hand over confidential information. In every case of impropriety, she stopped just short of accusing anyone of anything.

Yet four other staff had simply walked in, been asked the same simple questions, and walked out without so much as a funny look.

Two hours, just two hours, and his world and his department had been turned upside down. If this trend panned out over all six hundred employees, then hundreds of them would be suspect, thank god she only had a few days.

* * *

The CFO walked into Mendez’s office and requested his presence in the boardroom for an interview with the negotiator. Like everyone else called he was given no warning and no time for preparation.

His anxiousness increased as his meeting was only an hour after the chief accountant was interviewed, relaxing slowly as he remembered a dozen other people had also been interviewed. Coincidences happened!

Mendez walked into the boardroom calmly and confidently. The whole way up to the top floor he was practising his breathing exercises and going over his usual story.

The first man he saw on entering the room was the Human Resources Director. Normally Director Weber was the most intimidating man in the meeting, a man who regularly caused rooms to fall silent as he walked into them. Nobody ever joked in his presence, the man had no sense of humour period and went to sleep reading the company regulations. Yet the man looked beaten and defeated, a shadow of his normal self, and this was something Mendez quite frankly found disturbing.

The HR director was there as a neutral observer and to ensure employment law was adhered to, but his focus was elsewhere. Far from being concerned with the rights of his staff, he was mostly concerned with how his department and its procedures had failed the company he loved so badly. He was used to being given ultimatums by the board and CEO to bypass normal procedures and hire some people because of who they knew. But he had always assumed the procedures they had in place for hiring normal staff were sound, and like any good manager he was now re-assessing his whole world-view.

The fact that Mendez, the silky smooth, smart-talking broker from the eighteenth floor had just walked in, almost didn't register. Finally he met Mendez’s eye, and he rose from his slumped position and smiled, opening his notebook at a new page. This should be fun.

Mendez’s cool eyes moved from the HR Director and around the room, which was when he saw the other man, standing up close to the door. The new man was barely noticeable until he was, like a tiger in long grass was barely noticeable until you saw its teeth. He was motionless, his dark suit barely hiding his strong physique, and his blue eyes hard, taking in everything, and going over Mendez's slim frame and seemingly dismissing him as an insignificant threat.

This man was not one of the usual pretend thugs, those who hung around providing muscle and intimidation for gang bosses, or men like his stupid brother prepared to walk-the-walk for someone who would only talk-the-talk. No, this man looked totally professional, forged from years of military training and he exuded a demeanour that terrified him. He was in no doubt that this man was a trained efficient killer, one who had killed before, and that tucked under that Armani suit was a weapon, loaded and ready to be used.

In contrast to the two men, the woman negotiator seemed at first glance to be a kitten. Even pre-warned she was nothing like he expected, younger and even more beautiful. Her presentation was immaculate from her shoulder length brown hair, dark outfit, to her medium heeled shoes. At first glance that was all she seemed, until you looked closely.

The normal traits of a business person were missing entirely, there was no laptop, no briefcase or even documents in front of her, only something he at first thought was a pocket calculator, then saw was an actual dumb phone, one with a real keypad and tiny display. That alone told him that she had a personal assistant below her to run her affairs. He knew the brand, a Swiss one which eschewed practicality for privacy, one that was almost useless to anyone in this connected age.

To a fashion snob, such as Mendez, everything about her screamed influence and power. The Chanel tweed suit, the Cartier watch, the Gucci bag, any of which cost more than his car, not status symbols, but used for practical purposes and left hidden or obscured in a way that no movie star or socialite would dream of doing.

Like a high court judge the woman owned the room, her dark eyes stayed focused, analysing and processing with no thoughts of deferral until there was something more important.

Everything about her screamed she was totally in control of the other two men, and he suddenly found himself not looking at a young attractive woman, but at his executioner.

“Sit down Mr Mendez.” She said, indicating the seat opposite her and the HR Director, and one where his back was to the door and the bodyguard.

It wasn't a request, it was an order. Mendez took a slow breath, keeping his exterior look calm and professional. Despite his analysis she was still after all just another highly paid auditor who had had less than twenty four hours looking at the same data the current auditors had been sifting through for weeks. He sat down confident and relaxed, meeting the negotiator's eyes. She did the same, analysing him with her dark eyes, it was disturbing and he broke off contact before five seconds had elapsed, focusing instead on the room and seemingly giving the silent cue for her to begin.

“Tell me about your involvement in the company's loan agreements during the past twelve months?” She said without preamble or introduction and in a voice that offered no ambiguity.

Mendez relaxed, breathing out, so nothing about the Dubai companies..

“..Specifically your dealings with the two security exchange companies in Dubai,” the woman continued as she got up and walked over to a pile of documents.

“Please take all the time you need Mr Mendez.”

Oh god! The chief accountant must have said something, but the documents they had here were faked, and would back up their story so long as nobody actually tried to contact the fake shell companies.

His nervousness however must have shown on his face as the HR director briefly gave him one of his renowned thousand yard stares before writing something in his pad and glancing over at the back of the woman’s head.

The negotiator had gone to a very specific pile, the one he knew contained the same documents the auditors had already gone through. Even worse she had gone specifically to the folders containing the details of the Dubai companies. It was scary, the woman did not search or reference anything, she had gone straight to the correct pile and straight to the bottom two folders which she was now extracting.

“In your own time Mr Mendez,” she said again deadpan as she returned to the desk, ignoring the two new folders and looking again directly into his eyes.

He relaxed a little, it was just luck, a random pick, he knew every word and figure on both of them, but could that bastard Schulz have made some sort of mistake, one the original auditors missed but this woman had somehow picked up?

His mind went through what he had put on the falsified documents as he got his act together internally. Then he repeated his story, the one he had done with the other auditors and the one he knew by heart.

But the woman was still not even looking at the documents in front of her, hell she hadn't even taken her eyes off him once to glance down at them. Something did not feel right. His glances moved between the negotiator and the folders then to the HR Director whose gaze was deliberately averted from the two other people and focussed entirely on him.

The pregnant pause as he finished his spiel was more unnerving still. The next question surprised him again.

“The loan to Cayman securities four months ago was for how much?” She said it deadpan, again referring to nothing and not averting her gaze once from him.

“Er, I am not exactly sure, if I could grab the relevant notes I could certainly check the exact amount.”

“Thank you Mr Mendez, that will not be necessary.”

She turned to the HR Director, “I have no further questions for Mr Mendez at this time, he is free to leave. I will speak to Frau Hertzog next.”

The bitch did not even address Mendez directly, what the fuck did she want to know about the Cayman Securities loan, at least that one was legit. He breathed an internal sigh of relief looking at the HR Director, then back at the woman who was still staring at him, then back to the HR Director who finally nodded. He got up and without any further comment walked out relieved but still slightly nervous. Maybe he should get out now just buy the plane tickets and disappear, the money would go automatically to his accounts, but it might look suspicious if he moved too early.

His anxiety fell away like a sheet as he left the room and entered the open office area. Nobody was looking at him strangely, and the big scary guy had not moved a muscle during the whole twenty minutes he was in there. He checked his watch again, yes it had actually only been twenty minutes. He walked slowly back to the corridor and the elevator. He was going to the ground floor, he seriously needed a smoke and then he would grab that bastard Schulz and ensure he had not said anything.

* * *

The large blonde haired man slowly walked into the boardroom, dominating it, and causing the six men and two women to instantly go silent. The negotiator woman walked in confidently directly after him, along with two other men, one in the uniform of a police inspector. They all knew something had happened.

The blonde haired man took up his usual position next to the door after walking around the room, his stance indicated anything but relaxed, his eyes again taking in anything and everything except for the negotiator.

The uniformed policeman remained standing but the other sat down at a spare seat. The woman negotiator remained standing and moved to the focus of the boardroom table.

“These two gentlemen are from the Munich police, Commissioner Goebbels and Inspector Von-Gruden. I have asked these gentlemen here as three of your staff are currently being arrested for securities fraud and extortion. Only three men are involved but the fact they could do this exposes a massive weakness in your company's internal hiring and auditing practices. I have already presented my findings to the purchasers and I believe your negotiations are now temporarily on hold while they consider the future of the purchase and until these matters are resolved.”

The CEO stood up abruptly causing the bodyguard standing next to the door to flinch ever so slightly, his hand moving briefly towards his jacket breast. The CEO and everybody noticed and he kept his arms and hands in plain sight open and unthreatening.

“This is not possible. You cannot just accuse three of my staff..”

“Please be seated, Herr Mann,” she said forcefully, and in a voice used to being obeyed. A sense of fear washed through the room as she interrupted him causing a few people including the CEO to shake, the sensation dissipating as quickly as it came.

“The evidence has already been procured and presented to the police, and as we are all signatories to the same contract you understand all criminal matters uncovered will be reported to the authorities. I will not allow any argument. This matter is criminal under German laws and this supersedes all and any corporate decision.”

She paced along a cord cutting the partial circle of people. “You should be grateful that nepotism is not illegal as I find the level of incompetence of some employees, even some in senior positions, to be shockingly bad even for a company such as this. Nepotism and incompetence are however not within the scope of my contract, the ninety seven million euro hole in your accounts, on loans to fake Dubai companies is. The fact some of that money was likely destined to finance some sort of terrorism organisations also makes it criminal. You let that slip through, and as you may expect the purchasers are very unhappy.”

She continued her pacing. “My job as a negotiator is to neutrally present all the facts, this I have done. It was my recommendation to the purchasers not to continue the purchase without a further review of ongoing securities contracts and a review of your internal procedures.”

As she said the word “not” she could feel the level or ire rise in the room but totally ignored it and continued unbroken.

“The purchasers will contact you to let you know if they have accepted my decision or not. I now hand you over to Hr Commissioner Goebbels and Inspector Von-Gruden, who will explain your further obligations under German law.”

She waited a few seconds for the obvious reaction but it did not come, the negotiator was well aware the issues of this company extended all the way to the top, a few competent people were carrying the weight of a lot of incompetent ones, which never ended well, especially for a would-be purchaser.

The presence of the police and her bodyguard had however effectively muted any reaction, and the simmering thoughts from the people in the room remained just that.

Without further engagement the negotiator turned and walked out the door, the bodyguard falling in behind her without so much as a glance.

The five million dollars charged for her four days' services paled into insignificance against both the ninety seven million euro fraud, the criminal charges and the damage and publicity that would follow.

* * *

“Thank you Carl,” the woman said to the bodyguard as he fell in just behind and to the side of her in the elevator down. She had neither looked at nor acknowledged the man.

“I know it seems like you contribute nothing in situations like this, but you allow me to do my job without inference for which I am immeasurably grateful.”

“Nice suit by the way.”

The man’s demeanour remained unchanged, his focus remained on his surroundings; if there was a response to the woman’s complement it was undetectable, but she somehow remained satisfied by it.

* * *

Charlie lay in the soft bed, her arms outstretched and the back of her head buried up to her ears in the fluffiest down pillow ever, her brown hair wild like the rays of a badly drawn sun.

The real sun had just reached the top floor window hitting the back wall next to her bed. She was watching dust motes rise and fall in the still air above her, contemplating life, and enjoying the lazy time while she waited for the cafe on the promenade to open. She wasn't sure why she was waiting for this cafe to have breakfast, it just felt right.

As she focussed on a mote of dust, it would rise in the air until it left the shaft of sunlight and disappeared. She thought it was fascinating how so little could change their path, a breath, the heat from a body, even a thought seemed enough. Watching them was a meditation of sorts, like counting sheep, or a deep breathing exercise, and Charlie would consciously attach an irrelevant memory to each mote and watch as it floated out of her mind. Eidetic or not, the mind was not infinite, and the dream-fairy had to occasionally be called to chase out those unimportant memories.

The shaft of light disappeared, presumably behind a cloud and moved her attention to the present.

Time, she thought, was a strange phenomena! Waiting for a long-haul journey to end or sitting through a boring monologue, the seconds would slow as if dragged through treacle; yet at times like this, holidays with friends, the embrace of a lover, or the first bite of a dark chocolate, it would end far too quickly. In her mind it was as though the hormones controlling pleasure conspired to rob the brain of its timekeeping ability.

Charlie thought a lot, about a lot of things, and at a level most normal people would not comprehend. When it came to her thoughts of time and the human mind, she knew it was not like anything humans had yet designed, it did not tick at a certain rate, refused to be broken into instructions, there was no program counter, nothing at all recognisable we or Von-Neumann could understand. With the mind, like most other sciences, there was always more that was unknown than there was that was known. She thought about this now, as time was something you could never get back.

Her recent assignment to Munich was last minute, which was why she was now lazing in a hotel in Montreux and not one in Queenstown, New Zealand. Bethany was of course thrilled to get a last minute invite to the jazz festival. The two girls had had a great five nights, reminiscing about school days, dressing up, flirting with boys, and dancing to the great music. It felt like a fitting compensation to Charlie, redressing the balance of lost and free time.

Top hotels, like this one, always kept ‘spare’ luxury apartments for those who would pay, or for those exclusive guests they dare not refuse. Charlie felt privileged to be counted in both groups, and a simple call to the right person was all it ever took. It was a privilege she rarely invoked.

Like all good times it came to an end. Bethany flew home last night to Barcelona, and Charlie needed to return home to prepare for another assignment. Negotiators had a very special contract, one with a country and an ideal, and life would surely throw her plenty more curve-balls.

As she lay on the bed her internal clock told her another of those good times had come to an end too quickly. Charlie sighed, got purposefully up from the bed, and walked naked into the bathroom. Life went on.

* * *

Charlie knew that she spent far too long in the shower and far too long pampering her shoulder length mousy hair, and it took another fifteen minutes to brush and dry it. She was not vain, but presentation was everything with her work, the people she dealt with expected it, and years of finishing school made it feel routine.

The sun returned from behind its cloud, and was now almost touching the mirror she was using, and for a second her mind threatened to slip off on to another tangent.

She picked a loose white blouse, just because it looked nice in the sun, then a matching light brown skirt from the dozen outfits hanging in her wardrobe. She had worn both when she arrived, but both had been dry cleaned and lightly pressed since then and looked as good as if they had come off a rack in Prada.

Ignoring the formal fancy shoes in the shoe rack, she put on the same white Sneakers she wore the night before. This was as dressed down as Charlie was normally prepared to go, unless she was around a swimming pool or on a beach. But Charlie's life was far from normal.

After checking herself in the full-length mirror she slipped on the dainty little gold watch, a gift from a grateful client and wound it five times. Like the factory reset she had done to her phone before she slept, it was just a routine she got into.

Travelling was an art form, one she had mastered very early from her mother. She didn't exactly travel light, but she did travel efficiently; her luggage generally travelled separately to her, and she bought or hired as necessary, leaving wardrobes in countries she regularly visited.

Charlie had checked-in to find her clothes hanging in the wardrobe, pressed and ordered, and when she checked out, they would be packed and couriered home just the same. Anything she hired and liked she kept, if not she returned it. Walking around stations and airports and seeing people struggling with massive suitcases, she wondered why anybody did it any other way.

She packed her small toiletries bag into her little shoulder pack and zipped it close. Her phone was tucked in the pack’s side pocket, and without re-checking the room, why would she? She walked out to the lift. She sensed Carl rather than saw him. Carl was very good.

At precisely ten minutes before eight she dropped her room key card at reception, settled the invoice with a nod, tipped the staff who were especially efficient and friendly, slipped on her hat and sunglasses and exited the hotel.

* * *

The cafe she had frequented the last few mornings was maybe five minutes from the hotel and not quite open. She could see the lights were on, and the people inside, busy preparing to open, so walked into the cafe patio, and to her specially chosen outside seat.

The morning air was clean and smelled faintly of baking bread and cinnamon. The lake was flat, and the hotel behind the cafe held back the rising sun from the picturesque patio. The faint noise of the distant traffic and hum of a ferry’s diesel engine contrasted with the sound of birds in the trees, and the lapping of small waves on the shore. The overall effect was much more pleasant than it sounded.

The same waitress she had yesterday, walked out of the cafe and up to her smiling, it wasn’t a fake smile either, the girl liked her job and generally liked the clientele she served, she remembered Charlie.

“Good morning Miss, nice to see you again and such a beautiful morning, they say the weather is going to stay perfect for the rest of the festival.”

The girl delivered it in pleasantly accented French. She was young, tanned, quite pretty, maybe nineteen years old, and three or four years younger than Charlie. Her presentation would fool most but to Charlie it spoke clearly of a more working class upbringing rather than the posh private schools of Monaco, Cannes or Saint-Tropez which Charlie knew she was aiming to achieve. She genuinely wished her luck.

Charlie smiled back at the girl. “My last day in Montreux, but I wanted to end my visit on a high, in the nicest cafe in town.”

The complement got its desired response as the girl beamed. “If I remember yesterday, you and your friend both had cappuccino with cinnamon, and butter croissants. But, if I can make a suggestion, our best pastry chef only works on Friday and she has just baked some almond croissants, they are very popular and go quickly.”

“Then a large cappuccino and an almond croissant it is then,” Charlie smiled.

The waitress ticked her iPad with a finger and walked off, her wonderfully proportioned hips, that would cause most males heads to turn, swaying perfectly but unfortunately wasted on the wrong audience.

The next few minutes were wonderful, one of those times she thought of as she lay in bed earlier that she wished she could extend forever. However, it was now Charlie who was the mote of dust, being here and not New Zealand, staying last night, having breakfast here and not the hotel, they were all random drifts in her life. Nothing and nobody it seemed was immune to entropy.

Charlie's almost eidetic memory allowed many things to be recalled, but unfortunately the smell of the clean still air with waves of fresh coffee and baking, the coolness of the breeze on her skin and the crazy complexity of the vista meant it would never have the same effect remembered as experienced. People, she thought, lived, worked and struggled through life to experience moments like this.

The order came five minutes later, just as a casually dressed young man sat down in a seat two tables over. He would probably have sat at the table next to Charlie, but the cafe was still empty and it might be construed as forward or presumptuous to invade her personal space; well at least by a gentleman.

She recognised him from the day before, remembering that he had sat at the table she was now at, and felt a tiny bit guilty that she may have probably spoiled some sort of morning ritual for him, she smiled at his next thought it was always nice to be appreciated.

The croissant and the coffee looked and smelled to die for, she was thankful for her fast metabolism that helped keep her trim despite her somewhat sweet tooth, and as she briefly closed her eyes and savoured her first sip it totally took her attention from the kindly looking stranger.

Two female sparrows that had been hopping around the floor, flew up and landed on the back of the chair opposite her, eyeing the crumbs from the first bite of her croissant. A seagull landed close by on the ornate fence, impetuously waiting an opportunity to ambush either her, or the poor little brown predators after they made their move. She kept her attention on both as she still savoured the first bit of the moist flaky croissant.

“It doesn't matter how hard you stare at them, they won't go away,” said the young man casually in English as she expected, and just as all the birds flew off.

Charlie casually moved her gaze to the man, her cup close to her face as she inhaled, then took a small sip of her coffee.

He was smiling at her, his thinking first confused why the birds had flown off, then embarrassment for such a bad opening line, and finally relief at her smile which, he guessed correctly, was genuine. He turned away to hide the slight embarrassment and finished opening his small laptop; an almost comically stereotypical student favourite MacBook air in gold.

“You were here yesterday.” Charlie replied nonchalantly, placing her elegant white cup back on its matching elegant white saucer and giving him what she considered was one of her nicer smiles.

He turned his head back, relief on his face that she had replied and not thought him some weirdo. He wisely kept quiet, waiting for her to finish the sentence.

“I remember you sat in this spot, it really is a pleasant location, the views on a perfect day like today should really be savoured and appreciated.”

It was difficult for most women to tell if a man was hitting on them or just being friendly, and as she read him more fully she saw both. A Frenchman would have mentally undressed her by now, an Italian man mentally undressed her, bedded her, and would be waiting for her to cook him breakfast and meet his mother! This poor man had no time for any of that as he was mentally translating French to English, word-by-word, missing half of them and badly conjugating the verbs. Her smile almost turned into a small laugh, replying in French had been a little cruel.

* * *

Michael had come a little early so he could practise some more of his beginners French with the cute waitress, and, more importantly, without too many people listening. It had been a hard task engaging anyone in conversation, and he was initially a little disappointed when he saw someone at the table he had sat at yesterday. But his disappointment quickly turned to elation as he saw it was the same young attractive brown-haired woman who was at the cafe the day before.

When he saw her yesterday she was drinking and laughing with the black-haired, tanned girl who looked a little younger than her. He had originally tagged the pair as totally unapproachable to someone like him, as they wore expensive looking clothing, were deep in conversation in something he thought was probably Spanish, and both seemed well out of his league in the looks department; but here she was, the cuter one, alone, smiling, and absolutely beautiful.

Charlie in turn watched the young man going through his mental exercises. Young men were amongst the easiest to read, and this man was an open book. He wasn’t really her type, but she found she liked the casual yet unthreatening countenance he emitted, his pleasant dress and attitude and his nice smile.

She picked up that the suggestive way she was moving her fingers up and down the cup was having some small and unintended effect on the man, and stopped.

“As you are probably new to Switzerland, and being English, probably too polite to ask, I won't be offended if you want to join me. Though you do look like you’re kind of busy.” She pointed at the open laptop with the hand not holding the coffee.

Charlie had changed to English, a very easy and flawless English, Michael thought, accented in a way that eluded to a strata of society he could never attain to, but he was sitting in an area known for the rich, the famous and the glamorous.

His smile widened. “Thank you, that's very nice of you. I would love to join you.” He folded his notebook shut and pushed it into his little grey generic rucksack then walked the five metres to her table.

He put his hand out. “Michael James, from England and temporary resident of Montreux for the next few weeks. I enrolled myself in a voluntary fast-track French language course here a week ago, hoping to get a little bit more of the lingo before I start my new job. The timing was very deliberate because I really wanted to attend the jazz festival too.”

“Well, pleased to meet you Michael James from England! You can call me Charlie, also a temporary resident of Montreux.” She replied normally in French, smiling at him as his face fell. She could see the little cogs in his mind again processing the French and popping each French word in the sentence into its English one.

“Sorry, I almost got it,” he said sheepishly, apologising for nothing, the same way most British people tended to do. “But I really have only just started learning, and still really only at beginners level. I did understand most of what you said though.”

“So, Michael James, as you are learning, and we are having a conversation, what would you say to me next?”

“..In French of course!” She added cheekily, watching his face fall. Putting people on the back-foot was of course what she did and it was sometimes a hard habit to break with strangers.

Charlie waited, taking another bite of her croissant and secretly enjoying the mental discomfort she was putting the young man through. She thought it was strange he had not shown any such discomfort with the waitress when ordering his coffee, though to be fair, the waitress was all smiles, and a little flirtatious even for a French woman; she was unlikely to question his poor, bordering on terrible, use of the language if there was possibly a tip in it for her, and he was cute.

His deep thought evaporated slowly into a smile. “Thank you, you are very nice for letting me join you for your coffee.”

Charlie cringed at the mistakes, hiding her feelings well. The meaning was perfectly understandable, and the accent was not too shabby, for an Englishman. But she knew many French people who would use it as an excuse to change immediately to English, only to ironically murder the English language in the same exchange. She had no such qualms however, everyone started with training wheels on.

Hiding her thoughts with a smile, she dusted up a few crumbs and powdered caster sugar from next to the half-eaten croissant with her wet finger, again noticing how such as simple action could become suggestive to the male mind and laughing to herself how they kept reading things into every innocent action.

“Well, Michael James, that was.. interesting, but I guess we all have to start somewhere, I would have started by using the more familiar form of ‘you’ since we are after all sharing a coffee. Such familiarity may not work with an older or married woman, but for a young woman or one you had shown an interest in, it would be perfectly acceptable.”

He blushed a little, then she corrected his sentence and made him repeat it. He finished just as the waitress came back with his coffee. The waitress looked at Michael nodding slightly, then smiled and winked at Charlie. She said in French to Michael.

“Would sir like anything else?”

“Non, merci.” Michael replied in what, to Charlie, sounded like quite a normal local accent, and it was easy to tell there was no English to French mental conversion.

“Tres Bien, Michael. So, from Oxford to Switzerland, I assume you are going to be working at the European Organisation for Nuclear research as one of the researchers. To tell the truth there are not many other companies that will employ someone who cannot speak the language.”

Charlie rolled the sentence out in normal French and he looked at her strangely, working the words in his head, but totally failing. He fixated on the word Oxford in his mind. God she hadn't even had to use her gift for any of that.

“Sorry, my bad, Michael,” she laughed heartily after several painful seconds, “you spoke your last sentences in French with such nice pronunciation, I forgot you were still a noob.”

She hadn't of course, she never forgot anything unless she wanted to. But she was enjoying the innocent little game she was playing with the nice Englishman. Again she smiled and repeated it more verbosely in English, “I said I assume that you are working at CERN, an engineer, or some sort of scientist?”

“Yes, er, well a data scientist. And you mentioned Oxford, how did you know I came from Oxford, am I that obvious?” He said in English in a confused tone.

“A little bit of a guess really, you certainly don’t dress or act like any businessman I know, your laptop bag does say Oxford University on it, and the most obvious giveaway is that CERN is probably the only company in Switzerland where anyone could get a full time position without speaking the language, and it generally only employs smart people.”

“You do realise they generally speak English in CERN don’t you? Outside of the complex is a different matter but inside it is a bit like a different country, neither Switzerland nor France.”

“Have you been to CERN?” He asked enthusiastically, and giving up on his French for the time being. He had taken a sip of his cappuccino but had paused and was now maintaining eye contact with her, trying his best to act like a gentleman and not get distracted by the tantalising glimpses of flesh her loose blouse and knee length skirt offered.

She saw his thoughts, his face, his expression, his smile, his stance, his clothing and his voice; all were pleasant, all were complimentary, none were vulgar. Unlike most women, who looked at a man and fixated on the looks, then how rich the guy looked, (or the other way around in the case of her friendly waitress), Charlie read and summarised the whole package, and Michael had passed the first test.

She put down the half-full coffee cup, and slightly adjusted her skirt to show slightly less leg in case the inevitable distraction should occur. “I had a small tour of the facility with my mother eight years ago, a few years after the Higgs boson was discovered and just before the collider was closed for upgrades.” She didn't mention that the ‘guide’ was the director of CERN, and it was to route out a corrupt contractor, it was neither relevant nor appropriate in this conversation.

Michaels demeanour improved as the conversation returned both to English and his specialist subject. “Yes I was in my first year of high school when they announced the Higgs boson, I was always good at mathematics even back then, and now, twelve years later, here I am back at school again learning French; My high school French teacher would be shocked, I think she saw me as a bit of a disappointment.”

Michael unconsciously ran his hand through his hair and paused, his relaxed smile breaking through to Charlie as he finished his sentence. “It’s actually quite nice to see a beautiful woman take an interest in the sciences though.”

“Why thank you Michael, you are very flattering,” she said, putting down her almost finished coffee, “but I do take a rounded interest in most subjects. You are quite interesting yourself, a southerner who is not a southerner, with a MacBook that is not a MacBook. It speaks of an interesting history and some degree of intelligence, though the latter is a given considering who your employer will be.”

Her phone rang.

“Excuse me, I need to take this, only four people have this number, and one of those has just flown back to Spain, so it is probably important.”

She pulled a thin, narrow, black phone from the side pocket of her backpack, Michael could see a keypad and small display and was a little shocked as realised he was not looking at a smartphone. The woman then got up and walked a short distance almost out of Michaels hearing.

Charlie wasn't on the phone long, maybe thirty seconds, and he thought he caught the French words for ‘commissioner’, ‘Interpol’ and ‘Geneva’ before she hung up. She walked back in a sexy and sophisticated way that must have been trained from birth, then sat down and popped the phone back in the side pocket it came out of.

She sighed. “It does not matter how many bows and ribbons you tie something up with, there are always more questions, life was so much simpler in the past.”

“I heard that a lot from my grandmother, I hope it is not anything too serious.”

“No, just loose ends, reports, paperwork, the glue of bureaucracy that slows down progress, but nothing the office cannot handle.”

It certainly sounded a bit more serious if Interpol and a police commissioner were involved thought Michael, but he could have misinterpreted the whole exchange based on a few words picked out of a stream of overheard French. She could of course be a reporter, or even a writer researching a book, just what were this woman's secrets?

It was weird, she had done a lot of the talking, yet he realised he knew absolutely nothing about her apart from her name. There were so many questions, who was she? Why was she talking to or about a police commissioner? How did she know so much about him? Hell he didn't even know her second name or what nationality she was!

“You are a very interesting woman Charlie,” he finally said.

This really wasn't like Michael, he was generally much more in control of the initiative; now he could see the phone call, his short hesitation, and his bland compliment about how interesting she was, had given her the excuse to finish the last bits of her croissant and finish her coffee. He could feel the sense of a missed opportunity looming.

She pulled a green bank note out of a small purse that had appeared from nowhere, and placed it on the table, under the salt shaker.

“Anyway Michael, it was wonderful to meet you, while I would love to sit and talk more with you, I do have to get back to Geneva. Enjoy your coffee on me as an apology for taking your table and especially for you being such interesting company.”

He did not even think about arguing the bill, and as Charlie pushed her small purse back into the side pocket of the backpack, and, as she readied to leave she caught the disappointment in his face.

She hesitated a few seconds, maintaining eye contact, then pulled a business card out of her rucksack and handed it to him. “Maybe if you are ever in Geneva you can look me up and we can maybe grab another coffee. I will even let you pay. Though do not be too hasty to accept Michael, as I will expect all our future conversations to be in French. Think of it as just a little more incentive for you to learn.”

She winked more suggestively than was intended, got up, and put her small pack over her shoulder to leave.

Michael stood up also, trying to think of something, anything to say, and also hide his disappointment that she was leaving. He was both surprised and elated when she came in and air-kissed him gracefully on both cheeks. “Au-Revoir and Bon-Chance Michael James.”

She strolled purposefully towards the cafe exit, before he could fumble the right French word for goodbye, then he found he was too choked up to talk. Charlie didn't need to glance back, the man’s puppy-dog eyes, and his oscillating emotions said everything she needed to know. What she couldn’t understand though, was why she did it!

* * *

The tall well built blond haired man in the dark suit stood like a statue in the shadows of the trees and buildings close by and watched the encounter like an eagle, then slipped away to follow the woman.

* * *

Michael was, to say the least breathless, and to tell the truth, a bit shocked. He had never experienced trouble finding the next words, and usually had no problems talking to women, teachers and colleagues. People said he was gifted in that way, smart, funny, approachable, extremely intelligent and (his last resort) had some financial independence, courtesy of his grandparents inheritance which gave him a small house in Yorkshire he rented out to supplement his income.

This was the first time in his adult life that he had ever felt helpless and not in full control.

He looked at the business card he was still holding in his hand. It was a high-quality card, black, with the word ‘Charlie’ and an email address below it. Nothing else, he turned it over; blank.

He returned his gaze to the exit where she had just departed, hoping against hope that she would walk back, maybe run into his arms so he could smell her hair and feel her touch.. What! God he was losing it, those were the thoughts women had, not intelligent logical men like him!

He stared at the point she exited from for nearly thirty seconds until he realised he was still standing up, looking stupid, and his coffee was getting cold.

Finally he sat down, took hold of his rapidly cooling coffee and looked at the fifty franc banknote she had placed on the table. Switzerland was probably the most expensive country in the world, but two coffees and a Danish, came to nineteen francs at most. His confusion was shaken more as two sparrows jumped back up on her now empty chair opposite him, and a seagull landed heavily in the exact same spot it had been before.

* * *

Michael looked at the business card again for what seemed like the hundredth time; Two words ‘Charlie’, and an email address, it said nothing, and now he realised it said everything. He opened his notebook, a top end MacBook Air which he had re-installed with his favourite operating system and opened his web browser.

The website for the email domain was a faceless landing page unspecific to any area or business, it had the contact details and bio for a Mrs Alice Bortolusso, an executive secretary who had several titles after her name, none of which he knew. He entered general searches and backtracked company and domain registrations and got nothing but the same government PO Box in Bern. No news articles, no clients, no reviews.

He went through everything he could remember about the conversation, not trusting his memory and pulling out a paper pad and pencil, he always kept handy, to scribble the bullet points. That was when he realised that some of the things she had said were quite scary, ‘a southerner who was not a southerner’ He had moved from Yorkshire to South London when he was six! A guess? maybe one of the words he had said was said in his old accent.

But what about ‘a MacBook that is not a MacBook’ He had bought the Apple laptop for its amazing hardware, but how could she possibly know he had replaced its operating system? And the birds? For god's sake what was all that about? As they had flown away he remembered this sudden sense of danger at the same time, yet there was nothing, had they really just left her alone because she stared at them?

He spent a minute looking back at the birds and trying to out-stare the seagull as Charlie had done. The seagull ignored him and flew straight back when he shooed it off, same with the sparrows. He was shaken from his concentration by the waitress standing next to him and a slight note of concern in her voice. She had said something, but he did not know what. He smiled at her, replied it was nothing, then asked for a refill, all thoughts of the beautiful waitress now moot.

He wrote down how Charlie looked, how she was dressed, the phone type, the tiny gold watch, the backpack, which an image search said was either a fake, or a twelve thousand dollar Gucci. Despite having the most minimal sense of women’s fashion, he already knew the answer.

He wrote down a list of questions, underlined specific words, added times and the location, then closed both his paper notebook and his MacBook, mentally exhausted.

He looked again at the card, took a photo of it, as business cards were out-dated in his opinion, and realised he had a lot to think about before he sent that email. For some reason he now found the prospect of learning her language had moved from a chore to an enthusiasm; an enthusiasm he had not felt for years.

The biggest question though was why did Michael suddenly feel like a little puppy who had just rolled over and had his belly scratched!

* * *

Charlie, on the other hand, knew everything she needed to know about Michael James; It took neither an extensive google search or a lookup from any of the dozen agency databases she could call on, it took a few minutes over a coffee in a lakeside cafe.

Carl fell in next to Charlie as they walked to the large black Mercedes. She got in the passenger seat, realising how easily she could have delayed her return and enjoyed a little more of the man’s company, he was after all one of those rare men she felt comfortable with, and one who looked at the whole package, not just at the wrapping.

“Thank you Carl,” she said to the large blonde haired man. “I really appreciated your discretion on this trip, sometimes it is just nice to be able to walk around and behave like a normal person.”

The man smiled faintly. Carl, like most of the agents she had known, were not big talkers, but his loyalty was unquestionable. A thanks was the minimum he deserved for not spooking the people around her.

His surface thoughts never seemed to stray from his job when she was with him. He was pretty new, and exceptionally well built even for a bodyguard, having been assigned to her a year ago when she had been secretly appointed as negotiator in a very delicate matter between Russia and Norway. It was over a disputed border skirmish and had threatened to escalate badly as a lot of egos were on the line. It was also very unusual as in this case the Russians were not the aggressors. The two Norwegian border guards had been complicit in the rape, murder and cover up of the Russian female private who had strayed onto their territory.

Charlie had been impressed by Carl's single focus and professionalism and made a special request of the Norwegian defence secretary and prime minister to allow him to be assigned as her permanent bodyguard. The precedent had already been set before by her mother requesting the same of the British with Simon twenty years previously.

Carl was a very rare person, a lethal killing machine, one she had personally witnessed in action, and a person with a quiet, focussed mind, one possessing a deep sense of loyalty to the person he protected. Neither Charlie nor Gerty, had ever sensed any trace of maliciousness or deception from Simon nor Carl, and while other guards had come and gone, barely an hour in some cases, these two remained special.

Carl had replaced Harriet, who had been with Charlie since she was seventeen, and who had sadly left to start a family at the end of last year.

Charlie’s mind recalled the last assignment again, a simple acquisition that had gone awry and led to the uncovering of a massive case of embezzlement. They were getting called more and more into large business deals, ones directly involving Swiss businesses, and away from political and legal matters. It was within the contract to do this, but was getting more off the spirit of the original negotiator role, she would need to have a talk with her mother.

All her assignments were agreed on and assigned by a special committee called the negotiator liaison office in the capital, Bern. There was no published website, no business listing, no phone numbers you could call to hire a negotiator. Negotiators had a contract only to the Swiss government and to the Swiss people.

The outcome of their work could determine the lives of people or affect multi-billion dollar deals like the recent one; but they also negotiated contracts for top positions in government or industry where the integrity of the person was paramount, assisted police and Interpol and provided fair arbitration bound by the reputation of neutrality Switzerland so treasured.

They kept a small office in Geneva, mainly for appearance sake. There were two negotiators, Charlie, and her mother Gerty who was senior, but there was no nepotism as the two women were the only people who could actually do the job. There was one other employee in the office, Alice, a very efficient personal assistant, who like her mother was semi-retired, and who ran the negotiator’s affairs like a Swiss watch. The three women in Geneva working with the negotiator liaison office in Bern formed the entirety of the smallest but arguably the most important and unique department in the world.

# The Curse and The Gift

CHARLENE SCHNEIDER was born on January the third in the year two thousand, and like her mother she was born with an extra chromosome. It was a very rare defect and almost always resulted in a stillbirth, or by diseases like down's syndrome. However, in both her mother’s, and Charlie’s case, it had led to a differently wired frontal cortex, one responsible for both their marvellously eidetic memory and logical uncluttered minds.

It didn't end there though, the condition also led to their gifts, and, in Charlie's case, her maybe not so marvellous, split personality.

To know Charlie however you had to first know her mother Gerty, and also her grandmother Heidi, and for this we must take our story back to the second world war.

* * *

Heidi Bergmann was brought up by the Bergmann family after the war. It was a tormented and sad chapter in the history of the family. The Bergmann family had risen since the first great war to be one of the great industrial families in Switzerland. The senior Bergmann, had recently been made a Count, he lived with his beautiful wife in the largest and most prestigious castle on the shores of lake Geneva and had two sons, who both, to his great pride, had decided to become doctors. Unfortunately that was where the fairy story ended.

The eldest son, Gustov, had, against his family’s wishes, moved to Germany to complete his final years at the great medical university in Berlin. During these years he became a convert to the Nazi ideals, bestowing its leaders virtues, and spouting its propaganda to any of them who would listen. Then, and to his family's horror, he joined the Nazi party in nineteen thirty eight.

With the outbreak of world war two, he enthusiastically went to work on the war effort, getting posted to a secret medical research facility they were setting up just outside the city.

His letters home to his father at the time told of his great pride, and how he was going to be doing cutting edge research on the new field of genetics which would greatly help the cause and the war effort. His father burned all the letters in disgust, never answering any, and publicly disinheriting his son. Early in nineteen forty one, the letters dried up, and for four years the family heard nothing from Gustov.

Things were going bad for the Germans in nineteen forty five, they had been routed in Europe and the east, fighting was hand-to-hand in the German cities, and the regime's collapse was only weeks away. The family had all assumed Gustov dead, and were all set to wipe that chapter from the family history when Gustov turned up at his home bedraggled, malnourished and wounded. This was not the scary part however, the scary part was that Gustov returned home with a young girl, less than two years old, dressed in rags and as malnourished as the father, and who he claimed was his daughter.

The family was shocked and in a dilemma, here was their twenty seven year old son returned home with their grandchild, but he was also a deserter and probably a Nazi war criminal. The father would have none of it, the arguments and accusations started immediately, the son they knew was dead, a disgrace to the family. Gustov was just too weak to argue, and knew any attempt would only fall on deaf ears.

To protect the family’s now shattered reputation the father really had one choice, and the authorities were informed the same day. Their dilemma was however solved even before the father had returned, when Gustav, with his last strength, penned a short apology letter then shot himself in the head. The letter had one request, that the sins of the father were not the sins of the daughter and that they treat the young girl as a victim.

So the day the Bergmann family lost their son, they gained a granddaughter, a granddaughter whose name they did not even know. They called her Heidi after the innocent girl from the popular children's book.

The Bergmann’s younger son Kurt, was diametrically opposite to Gustov. Kurt had volunteered for the Red Cross during the last three years of the war, and had worked around the clock, treating both soldiers and civilians until its cessation. He returned to the family home on the north shores of lake Geneva to continue his medical studies, and to find he now had a young niece.

Heidi always had a simple life, but she had a form of downs syndrome, which was never diagnosed as she displayed none of the characteristic traits of the disease. People could however tell from an early age that she was not quite right; she never laughed or cried, she was too docile and never played with the other children, sitting by herself and shunning all company.

To look at Heidi though, with her beautiful face and ever present smile, nobody would know, she was the veneer of a normal beautiful child. Her intelligence was also never in doubt, for while she was reclusive with people, she took to books, and to music, especially the piano, very quickly, having an almost perfect recall of any book or tune she heard.

Without the science at the time, the reason for her problems was attributed to malnutrition, and whatever other terrors she had experienced in her first two years of life; it was said by many, that Heidi was God's way of making up for the horrors her father had committed, as by then the knowledge of the Nazi extermination camps and medical experiments were becoming public, and was shocking the world to its core.

The shame of the family was thankfully offset by the good deeds and brilliance of their other son Kurt, and the beauty of the music produced by Heidi. There was also the fact that no actual evidence against Gustov had yet been uncovered, in fact some records showed he had actually died in 1941.

Kurt had by then become a full doctor and began specialising in the new field of genetics, he was hoping to be able to help Heidi, a niece he had gotten very attached to. On his return to Geneva, he convinced his father to fund a new wing of the university to specialise in this new field. The new department, which he would eventually run, would help teach the next generation of scientists.

The family's second tragedy and miracle happened in nineteen sixty. It was when Heidi was seventeen years old, a year after Kurt had got married, and two years after he had become head of the new institute.

Heidi’s simple and trusting nature, her innocence and demure beauty, had not gone unnoticed by one of Bergmann senior’s business associates, a well known banker, who had a reputation for charity, but a secret dark side for underage children.

The people around Heidi, the family and friends who loved her, and those who were close to her, saw the change in the simple woman's countenance almost immediately. She began to shy away from men, becoming almost hysterical when left alone with one. The permanent innocent smile disappeared overnight, replaced by crazy mood swings and a pale sad expression.

Nobody, of course, had ever talked to the young girl about sex or babies as she was still mentally a child, and for eight months Heidi grew more, and more, fragile and reclusive.

It all ended the night she went into labour.

Heidi gave birth to a baby daughter and died an hour later from blood loss. The family were awoken at 5 a.m. by the screams of a child and the body of a troubled yet loved daughter.

* * *

Gertrude Bergmann was born in nineteen sixty one, and straight into the centre of the biggest scandal in Geneva. Though emotionally Heidi had only been a child, in the eyes of the law she was a fully grown woman, and just past the age of consent. Despite this, the anger felt by her family and the greater community over who could do this to the girl was visceral. The fact Heidi had not told anyone about it and, indeed, kept it so well hidden was considered enough not to push an investigation, and nobody was ever convicted or punished for the misdeeds.

Gertrude was nothing like her mother. Although she tested for the same extra chromosome as her mother, (science had improved considerably during these years), Gerty, as everyone had started calling her, displayed none of her mothers symptoms and in all ways she behaved like a normal, if not exceptional, girl.

She was immediately adopted by Kurt and his wife, and even when Freda finally gave birth to twin boys four years later, Gerty, being a girl, remained everybody’s favourite, she was in every way the perfect daughter and a great source of pride to the family.

Kurt and Freda noticed Gerty’s intelligence when she began talking at the age of thirteen months, it got very noticeable to everyone when by the age of three she had an almost full adult vocabulary in both French and German. Like her mother, Gerty was also beautiful, and loved music. She spent hours watching her step-mother, Freda, like a hawk, fascinated as she played the piano, then shocked Freda by reproducing a tune exactly with perfect recall. By six she could also converse with her English nanny in English, the Italian gardener in Italian, and would teach her step-brothers French and German.

Her father was reluctant to use his daughter as any kind of guinea-pig, but he sometimes secretly inserted control puzzles into her day-to-day play, and was shocked when she completed every one with ease. Her IQ was off the charts for her age, and her ability to recall facts bordered on eidetic. Gerty also told him she could forget things if she wanted to the same way she remembered them. It was untestable but for some reason he believed her.

Not wanting her to be considered a freak, as he had many examples from his position of where this had actually happened, he taught Gerty how to hide her abilities. He taught it to her as a game, to make the occasional mistake here or there or say she did not know, but as Gerty was about to go out into the wide world, it would soon become an essential life skill.

Gerty’s formal early education was at the local all girls private school, Surval Montreux, where she started as a seven year old, boarded for four nights a week and came home on the weekends, usually with a gaggle of new friends in tow. At the age of thirteen she was enrolled in Magdalen College in Oxford where she finished her studies in English.

Unlike her mother, Gerty was outgoing and assertive, she used the lessons her father had taught her to hide her exceptional abilities and would aim for second place in most subjects, considering it a failure if she got third or first. It was a lesson that would soon become increasingly important.

* * *

Gerty’s career in law was predicated by a single incident when she was twelve years old, and just when she hit puberty. Those six weeks were the most enlightening, and terrifying, of her life. Gerty recalled her memories exactly to Charlie as her daughter was nearing puberty and used her perfect recall to not hide any of the feelings or gory details.

Her friends went through puberty at different times, the all girls school was well prepared for this and very supportive, and even though Gerty knew it was coming, and had been given a million scare stories (girls would be girls after all) they all became secondary to the gift.

Gerty had been feeling more crabby as the day went on, she had just excused herself from class, her stomach was cramping and she had a bad headache. She decided rather than wait behind the three girls waiting to see the nurse, that she would go back to her room, a normally peaceful area she usually shared with a quiet Belgian girl called Mariah and try to rest.

Almost as soon as Gerty put her head on the pillow, she could hear the two girls next door arguing through the wall, she was annoyed, but not annoyed enough to go in there and inflame the situation more, instead she put her pillow over her ears and tried to concentrate on anything that would bring the noise down.

Instead Gerty heard another voice, it was the voice in her head, the internal monologue that her father told her everybody had, and that preceded the actions you would do or what you would or would not say. But this was different, her voice was saying two things at once, and one of them was in Italian!

It only took her a few seconds to realise it was the two girls next door, but the sound was not their voice and it was not coming in through her ears. Her moment of panic faded very quickly into fascination.

[Just go, leave the cow, let her have the lipstick, you’re better than this] she heard a few seconds before a door slammed, then the sound died down to nothing.

But the same internal voice was still there. [I can get some more lipstick, if I ask Mama she will get me one and I can replace hers, it’s only stupid lipstick]

She had dug her head out of the pillow by now, but she heard the voice just as clear.

[maybe I should admit to it and apologise..] It was the Italian one.

Gerty moved off her bed and sat on Mariah’s bed, the other side of the room, as she did this the monologue in her head started to fade and another English one faded in [Was Hercules Greek or Roman, maybe leave that out, OK..]

Weird!

Her concentration broke off as a stomach cramp hit and she went back to her bed and lay down. She was obviously dreaming, but she wasn’t. You did not realise when you were in a dream, but you most certainly knew if you weren't. This was stupid, magic was bullshit, it was not possible.

She closed her eyes and cleared her mind again, the Italian voice faded slowly in. It repeated the same thing over and over again [maybe I should admit to it and apologise..] like it was caught in a loop.

She needed to know, and walked purposefully out of the room, forgetting about her stomach cramp and headaches completely. She got to the next room and knocked on the door.

[What] “What” [Do] “Do” [You] “You” [Want] “Want” [Just tell whoever it is to piss off, yes that English word was good and it’s not as bad as fuck off..] It was so obviously Sara.

Gerty opened the door, [who the hell] “Piss o..” [oh damn it's Gerty] “..Oh it’s you Gerty, sorry about the noise, Fran and I were just having a row, she thought I took her lipstick.”

Gerty caught the whole underlying thread, every single thought, accepted or discarded before it came out of her mouth, almost like an echo, it was very distracting. [What's she here for? I hope she is not angry with me, and why is she staring at me like that]

“Let's sit down Sara,” said Gerty in a calm and what she thought was an assertive, motherly tone. She felt Sara’s acceptance and she sat on the bed next to her, dimming her concentration as her thoughts got louder and became emotions.

It was obvious to her now, that she was somehow reading Sara’s mind, and the other voice in her head had been Fran’s. Sara’s ongoing thoughts spilled into Gerty’s mind, like one of those new big scrolling message displays at the stadium. The thoughts were a mixture of confusion, and a little bit of fright, as she saw how Sara saw Gerty as one of the more popular girls, who could make Sara’s life hell. Up close she sensed Sara’s emotions welling up, threatening to do the same to her.

She took Sara’s hand. “Fran is your best friend, you know that?”

Sara’s emotions surged in Gerty’s head and she turned it off by concentrating on the pain in her stomach, she couldn’t turn it completely off, but she damped it to an acceptable level. Sarah was nodding, threatening to cry.

Gerty continued. “It would be silly to ruin a beautiful friendship over a silly lipstick, that someone as beautiful as you don't even need. Fran is more upset because you lied.”

[I didn’t lie..]

“I know you are telling yourself you didn’t lie, but both of us know that isn't true. Lying is not the problem Sara, I lie every day, everyone lies, but not to their best friends.”

[She can’t know but I don't want her or Fran as an enemy] Gerty picked it up clearly, touching her hand seemed to clarify the already clear thoughts more.

“Fran loves you, but she’s hurt! You need to apologise and make up. Even if you did not do it, you can see that an apology is necessary. Nobody else needs to know and I will ensure Fran keeps it secret until you are both ready to laugh about it later, which you will.”

During the talk she felt Sara’s emotions change, the feeling of guilt and betrayal rising, then relief, as she knew Gerty was right.

Gerty wrapped her arms around her the second before Sara burst into tears. She held her tight for a minute listening to a whole raft of worries and emotions as they flooded her mind, mainly they were ones that said Fran would not accept her apology.

“I’m going to get Fran and have the same talk. Remember when she walks in here in a few minutes time, she is your best friend you wronged with a tiny, silly mistake. I guarantee she will forgive you, if not you can move in with me because I know deep down you are a nice person.”

Poor Sara was a mess of emotions.

“Remember, ten minutes, you can then both make up, have a good cry and bring her around to my room for a cappuccino..”

Sara’s emotions faded as Gerty walked out the door.

She found Fran on the stairwell also crying, her thoughts worried that she had accused Sara for nothing. Gerty used the same analysis as Sara on Fran, promising her she was the wronged party and Sara really wanted to apologise for being a bad friend.

She led Fran to the room and gave her a hug and a nod. Fran had barely opened the door before Sara was on her hugging her and saying sorry so many times and how stupid she was. She left them like that going back to her room, her endorphins flowing so much it overwhelmed both her stomach cramps and her headache.

She experimented with it more, how to turn it up or down. There was no sense of direction like sound, but the level increased the higher the concentration or closer somebody was to her, like a room full of people she heard the ones the loudest or closest the best, at two or three metres there was perfect clarity, including vague images, but as they got more than five or six metres distant the images would fade and it would become very difficult to hear. After ten metres there was nothing. Unlike sound, physical objects in between had no effect whatsoever.

Sara and Fran were not an isolated case, she mediated several other little issues and her popularity soared. So many girls wanted to be around her and friends with her, and knowing which of those were genuine, and which of those were for status was almost unfair. A couple of the more superstitious girls put around rumours there was something sinister, that Gerty was a witch, but nothing stuck as Gerty was never cruel with anyone. She did however wonder if the rumours were indeed true.

The big high lasted only ten days, and was soon shattered by the same hubris.
