The history teacher was standing by her when she was discussing the mid-term paper set for tomorrow, and the students needed to learn all the history of one of a number of Greek, Roman or Egyptian cities, as the test would only be one of these. Unfortunately at the time she was thinking about the main question on the history of Pompey and its destruction.

Gerty let slip to a group of girls who were anxious about the test, that it would probably be Pompey. It was a big mistake.

Word soon got around and at the end of the test, when some girls were patting her on the back, she knew there would be repercussions. They came fast and they came hard. Gerty was accused of cheating and after a brief meeting with the history teacher, the headmistress and her father she was temporarily suspended for the three weeks remaining of the term.

* * *

A very moody and unhappy Gerty was confined for a week to her bedroom, convinced that everyone was against her. Reading her parents' minds produced nothing but sympathy for her being expelled, but the evidence against her was damning, and both thought her guilty despite everything she tried to say. Unfortunately the reality became more dangerous than the lie.

Gerty’s denial, and attitude, got so bad that her father eventually and reluctantly engaged a psychologist he knew of. It was a massive and near fatal turning point in her life, one she was almost not prepared for. She knew from her mother’s history that some men were predators, and the thoughts she was now getting from many of them when she got too close to them backed that up, most men were horrible and disgusting, even the nice ones.

However nothing prepared her for the psychologist she was about to meet. The man’s thoughts were terrifying, what he said, and what he thought, were two different things. His thoughts were of the money he would make from her family, and how, once he had control of her, and had her institutionalised, how he would fuck her every day, until maybe a little accident like the others. Oh how he loved to see them plead.

She knew the word fuck, and, jokingly, what the act was from the other girls, but the pictures that accompanied the thought into her mind, as he sat on the seat opposite, close enough for her to smell his disgusting breath, was both shocking and repulsive.

She was scared, very scared, something buzzed in her mind, quickly suppressed by the fright she felt. Her first guttural reaction to him almost played into his hands.

“Oh my! There do seem to be some deep seated trust issues here, Mrs Bergmann,” he said as Gerty moved further from his breath and the cesspit that was his mind.

“You should not frighten her, there is no real reason to be so close to her. She is never like this with anyone else so please move back and give her some space.”

The man’s thoughts turned on her mother. Gerty had never felt so much anger well up in her in her life as all the while he smiled and leaned back into his chair and made a note in his diary. She knew the marks he made in the book was gibberish and meant nothing to either him or anybody, but fooled people that he was smart, that it was a private shorthand to protect privacy.

Her mother was of course with her for the session, silent and sitting to the side. In fact it was her mothers voice and presence that helped keep her from running out. The only thought at the top of her mothers mind was a deep concern for her only daughter, and a growing dislike of the man.

But Gerty’s logical mind was already processing the way out, and she took in her situation without emotion and suppressed the elevating hormones and anxiety she had felt. The talk with the psychologist was by far the worst ordeal of her life, and made the interview with the headmistress seem like a walk in the park. It was like a fencing match with a scorpion.

She won, but in truth it was very close. She did not fall for any of the man’s trick questions, had plausible excuses for the cheating incident in which she admitted to eavesdropping, thank you for sharing those thoughts mother! And, after twenty five minutes her mother stood up and terminated the session. Gerty’s relief was palpable.

By this time Gerty was becoming both an attractive and refined girl, and this was not the last time she would encounter such people, mostly men, but sometimes to her shock, women.

She never went back to the girls school, taking three months sabbatical at her step mothers request, and finally being enrolled at the school in Oxford. She was very sad to leave the school in Montreux, it held so many good memories despite the one bad one, but she adapted, making dozens more new friends in the new school. Again Gerty kept her profile low, remembering the lessons from her father and the recent incident, it had convinced her never to show off her gift and never to display any overly-exceptional intelligence.

After school in England Gerty earned an easy law scholarship to the university of Zurich, specialising initially in legal, then international financial law. She met with the worst excesses of society and soon learnt to suppress her emotions, it was the only way she could get through the day.

She progressed up the legal tree from lawyer to barrister and finally to the youngest ever high court judge. In every single case she refused to prosecute anyone she considered innocent or defend anybody she considered guilty, as a barrister it was difficult to choose if you were prosecutor or defender, these were the only times she used her connections. As a judge it was even more difficult, money always bought better lawyers, but she would use her power to even the playing field in all cases.

Gerty married Johan Schneider, Charlie's father at the age of thirty eight and had Charlie at the late age of forty. Johan was a free spirit, ten years younger than Gerty, dashingly handsome and rugged. Gerty had almost given up on men, the few that passed her normal tests did not excite her as a woman and those that excited her as a woman had too many ulterior motives.

Johan had been a mountain ranger, ski guide and volunteer rescue crew for the ski patrol, he was a bit of a loner, his whole world had collapsed four years before he met Gerty when an avalanche took out his village killing his whole family except for an elder sister in Canada. If not for that fateful day that Gerty had gone off-piste, crashed into him, and twisted her ankle, he would still have been living in his small house high in the mountains.

For the two hours it took him to get her back to the resort, she felt nothing from the man but concern for her safety and a tinge of sadness. As they got to know each other further, his thoughts diverged thinking of her intelligence and beauty, and a sadness that a woman such as this, could never love a man like him. Gerty of course fell instantly in love.

She met him every day she was not working, appreciating his quiet and unassuming mind almost as much as his gorgeous body and the five years between 1997 and 2002 were the best years of her life.

He died in a skiing accident in 2002 well before Charlie got to know him. Charlie’s only unformed memories were through that of a two year old, the family pictures she had, and thankfully of course those memories her mother later shared.

Gerty also never forgot the psychologist, nor the circumstances surrounding her mothers death. During her two years working at the Attorney General’s office in Lausanne, she directed investigations against several high profile people, including the psychologist who was convicted of three cold-case murders.

She also re-opened a dozen other cold cases, working with Interpol to eventually undercover the largest paedophile ring in Europe and taking down many high profile predators. Unfortunately, though the name of her biological father was eventually uncovered due to DNA evidence from another case, the man himself had died over twenty years before, murdered by an unknown assailant. For the first and only time in her life, Gerty wished the murderer well.

With her reputation for thoroughness and attention to detail, her mother was always in demand. After helping her step-brother Frank, by then a senior detective at Interpol, solve a number of high profile cases, she began working in the government uncovering corruption and fraud.

Gerty was appointed Switzerland’s second only negotiator, a position vacant for almost five hundred years, by a one hundred percent majority of the special negotiator committee; her powers were wide-ranging and unprecedented in any modern democratic government, she also rarely used them.

* * *

Charlie’s development largely mirrored her mothers up until puberty. It was thought she had little of the musical talent her mother had, but it was just that she was not really interested except for one brief insight. At the age of twelve, and after going with her uncles and her cousins to the concert that same week at the ‘Stade de Geneva’, she replicated one of Coldplay's better known songs on the piano perfectly to a group of friends, unfortunately her voice was terrible.

Her step-grandfather, Doctor Kurt Bergmann, also known as Count Kurt Bergmann once he assumed the head of the family, died when she was only ten years old. He had gone from renowned geneticist to lecturer to guest lecturer and because of his daughter’s special condition, he had spent his whole life in this area of study.

By the time Charlie was born, Kurt was in his late seventies, and was long retired from his profession, having never found the answers as to how the mutation in both his daughter and Charlie led to such a massive increase in the processing power of the mind. He kept all his research reluctantly secret, only ever exposing research which could be reproduced outside of his daughter and granddaughter.

Grandpa and her mother would take young Charlie everywhere, she had no need for school, spending her time accompanying her family to the great cities and institutions of the world, personally being tutored by two of its most intelligent people. She accompanied them as they talked and dined with the rich and powerful, the great and noble and became good friends with some of their children. At the age of twelve, she was better connected than most royalty or movie stars, spoke seven languages and had an undergraduate understanding of many subjects. Her only formal education was eighteen months at an English finishing school at sixteen which felt more like a vacation than an education, but which she enjoyed immensely.

With her mother to guide her through the transition, and Charlie’s seemingly greater control she had on her emotions, puberty was an enlightening rather than a frightening time. Even as her mother shared her crueller encounters and those of her time as a high court judge, Charlie accepted and processed them like any other. Several memories were so shocking she had trouble believing these people existed. But they did. And as she moved out into the world she accepted it was a broken place and with her mother decided as a negotiator she could also do something to help. She accepted the role on her nineteenth birthday.

Her mother had also taught her one other great and important lesson. Anonymity. With the gift, Charlie’s ability easily exceeded her mothers. The level of concentration she required was much less than her mother and the range a little greater, but Charlie had two other abilities that her mother did not have.

The first ability she found out by accident with her uncle’s pet dog, Butch, and that was that weaker minds could be influenced.

She remembered it was not long after her gift had shown itself when she was walking Butch around her uncle’s estate. She enjoyed walking Butch around the large open grassland and taking him down to the shoreline to have him fetch sticks. On this occasion she was walking through the long grass with him when she got a weak impression of danger. Simultaneously, or just after, the dog began to bark, pulling on his leash. From about twenty metres away, she heard a scream and a man and woman’s head pop out of the grass, both looked to be naked and she averted her gaze to the dog adding a strong sense of humour at its silly behaviour.

The dog stopped barking immediately, going unnaturally calm and looking between Charlie and the panicked couple. She reached out and sensed the shame from the woman and the frustration from the man, turning away quickly and taking another route down to the shore.

Charlie repeated the experiment and found that any animal in a heightened state of danger, anger, hunger or weirdly lust could be sent a simple emotion. The thoughts for safety and danger, were the only ones she found that she really needed.

It was a weak sense of danger that she projected at the birds in the cafe that day with Michael; she had also noticed Michaels brief flinch as he looked around for danger. Humans were basically animals and felt the same primal emotions too, but emotions processed through a more complex mind rarely produced the same results.

Of course her other ability was the crazy part of her she was not proud of and liked to forget existed. She had been very panicked and frightened the first time she found it out.

* * *

Charlie remembered the day it happened as easily as she remembered anything she purposefully wanted to. She was a frequent visitor to the university library, her mother gave several seminars a year for the law faculty, and as a young child Charlie had gotten to know many of the staff there and often hung-out while her mother lectured.

She had heard mothers particular lecture the year before and took the opportunity to re-read The Magic Mountain by Thomas Mann. She had read an English translation on her e-reader, but the library had an original German edition.

She had tried to use her concentration to dull some noise from the workman in an adjacent room when a large rat ran out from beneath her table. Her small scream was impulsive. With the gift, Charlie was not used to surprises and every head in the library turned, looking down and around for the source, only the two people quietly reading at adjacent chairs and who had heard her actual scream had turned her way. She shrunk back into the book guiltily.

[...] [...] [...] [...]

The voice came into her mind clearly, as though from a person a few metres distant, it sounded differently to the way people did when she read them. Normally everyone had the same voice, a voice which was the same as her internal monologue and in the language they thought in. This internal voice was different, deeper, older, androgynous, like static or crackling mixed with words that were incomprehensible.

The two people who had turned her way, and everybody else in the library had gone back to their books, phones or laptops. She opened her mind, two voices came in, one was parsing ancient Gaelic, the other thinking about lunch, she opened even further, a dozen internal voices, all the same assaulted her senses. Then they all unexpectedly dulled..

[...] [...] [...] [...]

Charlie closed off her concentration, a sense of panic, she got up, the book discarded and falling to the floor, she looked around again, both the people close by her eyeing her as discreetly as they could, the man was procrastinating whether to ask if she was OK.

Charlie chose this particular spot as it was on the highest level and had only three tables within a ten metre sphere, but maybe she had missed somebody. She looked urgently around, up and down, there was nobody.

[...] [...] [...] [...]

She panicked again and ran down the stairs and towards the exit, several sets of eyes followed her progress, all with either worry or concern behind them. As she burst out of the doors, Charlie saw her mother running towards her along the corridor, and ran towards her.

“Charlie!”, she shouted and ran to her, the two making the distance in a few seconds.

“Mama!” She cried and ran into her arms.

“What is wrong Charlie? I felt your panic across the building!!” Her mother’s hands and face moved all over Charlie examining her then looking for danger.

“A voice Mama, a strange voice, it talked to me!”

Her mother looked around again with raised concern, then when she saw nobody within fifty metres and only two other young girls further away, she turned back to her daughter, cupping her face in her hands. “You’re safe Charlie, there is nobody here, just me.

[...] [...] [...] [...]

“It's there again Mama! A voice in my head. Can’t you hear it?” She screamed looking everywhere, her head finally burying itself in Gerty’s bosom.

The panic in her daughter's voice terrified Gerty, who like Charlie had become accustomed to being in total control, knowing people's intentions and aware of all that was happening around her. Yet as hard as she tried to concentrate, all she got were the overwhelming emotions and internal voice of panic from Charlie.

“I really don't hear anything Charlie, but I can see you are genuinely afraid of something. Hopefully it was some sort of waking dream, maybe you nodded off to sleep.” Gerty got out her phone.

“Yes Simon, can you bring the car around to the front of the library building immediately, Charlie is not feeling too well and we are leaving.”

She remembered Mama making another two calls, first to the family doctor to meet back at the chateau, and the second cancelling the rest of her lectures for the week, due to a family emergency.

The drive home was just Charlie and Gerty in the back seat, her face now buried in her mothers lap, but her tears dried up. Charlie was much better at closing off her emotions than Gerty had ever been and Gerty was actually genuinely shocked by how powerful they were; she had felt Charlie's mental outburst so clearly, as it seemed had half the students attending her lecture.

She was not sure what the repercussions of it would be, they were in a great seat of learning full of some of the smartest people in the world, one or more of them would talk and ask questions. From Gerty’s own research and the talks she had had with her father and others, she had heard of the terms shared and mass hallucination, she was even now working on her excuse for herself to leave the lecture and remove Charlie. Though she knew her new position would keep anything from ever coming her way.

At home they were met by the family doctor, an old student of Charlie's grandfather, a renowned surgeon, now semi-retired and his private practice catering to just a few exclusive clients.

The voice did not talk to Charlie again that day, and her, and her mothers panic abated as they sat together talking it over logically. First with the doctor, who, not being given anything solid to go on, had prescribed a mild sedative and rest, then just the two of them.

Charlie fell asleep on the sofa in the lounge, with Gerty staying next to her all night. Charlie did not wake up until almost midday and woke to her mother reading a book next to her.

“Good morning, sleepyhead!” Gerty said smiling, I brought a blanket and pillow down as you looked too tired to go upstairs. Let me go and see if I can get you some toast and a coffee.

“It was real Mama,” Charlie recalled quietly, “but it was so different from the normal memories I receive, normally I hear those in my voice, but this was somehow different.”

Charlie and Gerty actually had no real need to speak verbally, but hearing each other talk was a much more personal thing than listening to the other person speak in your head in your own voice. They had learned this out of necessity, and while they could not turn it off any easier than turning off your hearing, they had both perfected tempering down their concentration to a minimum, so that even someone standing next to them could fade to a background hum. They used it often when they were both present for a negotiation to communicate privately, but at home and together as mother and daughter it was always spoken French.

They were both sitting calmly in the library, a large quiet room taking up nearly a quarter of the ground floor of the east wing.

Though Gerty had been born into wealth, she had also accrued a considerable fortune herself. Their ‘chateau’, was actually a small castle, with extensive vineyards close to her old family home, that she bought opportunistically in the mid nineties as a long term investment. With the death of her step-father, and the establishment of the negotiator position the year before, Gerty relocated to the chateau, to give her more privacy for her work as a negotiator.

She and her step-sister Sam, the new baroness, were good friends and Gerty entrusted the maintenance and cleaning of the chateau to Sam, who ran the family estate. It was a very amicable arrangement and allowed a skeleton staff to run the chateau and call on help as required. It also had the benefit of lot’s of visits from Sam, and not too many noisy mind’s disturbing them.

Their housekeeper Mimi brought a large pot of earl grey tea, and after looking worriedly and sympathetically at Charlie, left.

“Can you share with me what you heard, Charlie?” Her mother said after pouring the tea. The two sat on old stuffed armchairs adjacent to each other with the small coffee table in front and Gerty passed a cup to Charlie. Charlie blew on the cup, then breathed in the aroma, finally taking a short sip.

[...] [...] [...] [...] she recalled exactly.

“Oh it is a bit weird,” replied Gerty, “what are the strange static like hisses surrounding the words, most languages are a derivative of older ones, this sounds, I don't know ancient, guttural, one of the words sounds a little like “dragon” in ancient Greek, the rest is noise.”

“I was thinking maybe it is a language I don't know, but I cannot even pronounce the words with my voice, never mind spell them or type them into a translator.”

“I think you are on to something there,” Gerty said, after taking a sip of tea and pondering it a little more, “but you are right, I remember once being close to this old woman whose thoughts were in some sort of ancient Hindu dialect, but with her I could at least repeat the words to somebody later. These do not feel like proper words, but words mixed with something else our mind cannot process correctly.”

“Did you detect any malevolence at all?” Gerty continued.

“Nothing Mama, it had no emotional inflection attached at all, it meant nothing, the sentence was just there fully-formed in my mind. I cannot explain, but it did feel like someone was talking to me. Also, the other voices around me got dimmer, like it took priority. I did not want to answer. Maybe he somehow sensed I was afraid and stopped.”

“You say he?” She said immediately, her sharply honed legal background zooming in on the inconsistency.

“I don't know Mama, it was an assumption I guess, it could be she, it or them..” Charlie trailed off not wanting to dig further as each added another layer of mystery.”

“Hmmm,” said Gerty, “it or them! Well we can probably exclude the ‘them’ option and probably also the ‘it’.” They both went quiet again for a few seconds.

“What if,” Gerty said slowly again, “what if we look at this logically, like scientists. I was as scared as you when I first got the gift, we are always frightened of what we don’t know, and the best way to get over this is to understand it.”

Gerty got up. “Yes Charlie, you have gotten over your initial shock and you said yourself that you detected no malevolence, maybe we should try and reproduce this under experimental conditions with us taking the initiative, maybe try and open up a dialogue if possible and learn.”

Charlie sighed, resigned, “I had already come to this conclusion Mama, I think my fear was primal, the rat scared me a lot, then the rest was the fear of the unknown.”

Gerty sat back down and put her hand out across the arm of the chair to take her daughter's hand, “I am proud of you Charlie, you know that don't you?”

“I know Mama, so long as you are here I will always feel safe.”

It was settled, Charlie would try and replicate the experiment the next morning, with her mother present, and after the effects of the sedative had worn off.

Gerty retained three people full time at the chateau; Simon, who was an ex-soldier from the highland regiment, who also doubled as their driver and protection detail. Mimi, Simon’s long-time partner and their housekeeper, and the old groundskeeper Aurelian. All the staff were well paid and totally trustworthy. The next day, after breakfast they asked the staff to keep out of the east wing so Charlie could have some privacy and quiet after her little episode.

“If you do this we need to think about what you want to say. I wrote down some bullet points while you were sleeping, ones I usually use when I am looking to find the truth. I know you cannot understand it, but it may understand you.”

Gerty laid down a small sheet with five questions and space to write on, the pen and paper was for Gerty in case she could not read her daughter. The questions were simple, who, what, where, why and how. Next to the paper was a vial. Gerty had asked the doctor for a syringe and a sedative in case of emergency, and Gerty had enough medical training to administer it if Charlie looked like she was in distress.

“I am going to initially stand at the end of the room, whatever you did yesterday was very scary and I felt it two buildings away..”

“No, don't leave me Mama, please!” Charlie said.

“OK, maybe..” Gerty reluctantly sat back down and held her daughter's hand, smiled and nodded at her that she was as ready as she could be.

Charlie cleared her mind and tempered down her gift as much as she could, taking a few minutes to get to a state of total calm, then she brought the thought forcefully to the front of her mind like she did when sending an emotion to an animal.

[we will talk now]

Gerty shuddered, gripping Charlie's hand tight, then relaxed, it felt to her like an ice plunge or when the dentist had hit a nerve.

[...] [your] [...] [is] [...] [dragon will not] [...]

[I do not understand] Charlie replied almost immediately.

[...] [your] [...] [is] [...] [dragon will not] [...]

“It is talking to me Mama, I understand some of the words now, but it is still mostly that horrible static.”

“OK, Charlie, the good news is you are controlling this, not the other way around, and remember we only fear the unknown. It is your job now to ask the questions so that the unknown becomes the known, and your fear is replaced by logic and truth.”

Her Mama’s words were prophetic and Charlie felt herself getting under control.

[Who are you?] she thought.

[...] [your] [...] [is] [...] [dragon will not] [...]

“It is no use Mama, it is just repeating the same.” Then she shared it again with her mother.

“Try a little more forcefully, with a tiny bit of anger,” her mother said, a different emotion may give a different response.

[Who are you? Is that your name, dragon?] she thought a little harder and what she thought was a little angrier.

“I hear you Charlie,” her mother shouted. “Your voice in my head has changed though, it is different.” Her mother was excited, not scared and it added to Charlie’s sense of being in control.

[I am you] [...] [...] Came the Delphic reply.

Ahh, her mind processed, it was not a person then. She had discussed psychology with both her mother and grandfather, she knew about split personality disorders, waking dreams and other manifestations, was this it? Was just another part of her brain, it would make sense why her mother could not hear it if that was the case. She skipped two questions.

[Why are you talking to me?]

Her mother squeezed her hand. “You changed the question honey, did it reply? I am concentrating now but I heard nothing.”

“The voice said it was me Mama, it was very cryptic.”

She squeezed her hand in acknowledgement.

[I was] [...] [...] [woken, I listen]

[You listen, to what?]

[To you] [to] [...] [...]

Charlie repeated the confusing replies to her mother.

“You are doing good honey, your facilities are back under control and your breathing and heart rate have gone down, just keep asking questions it seems neither of us are in danger..” Her mother thought then added another question onto the notepad with the pen, ‘Am I in danger?’.

Charlie went with that one first.

[Am I in any danger?]

[...] [I do not know]

[I mean in danger from you?]

[No]

She wrote NO next to the question and felt the visible relief in her countenance as she read it, then she released her mothers hand, smiling.

“I think I have this under control Mama, it must be another side effect of the gift that is causing a part of my brain to talk to itself, maybe the fright I had yesterday broke some barrier or made some connection. Whatever this dragon is, it is certainly not that scary anymore.

She broke the connection with the other part of her brain with casual abandon, and felt her life return to normal.

# The Mistress of Chaos

FIVE MEN SAT AROUND a large stone table, in an imposing room, a room whose architecture and decoration defied any style. Dense lush rain-forest dominated the scenery out of the large darkened windows, water dripping down as it condensed on the thick glass. The walls were stone, roughly finished volcanic basalt, except for one wall that was smooth, plastered, and embossed with the image of a monster. A monster straight from hell.

The whole scene seemed oddly out of place, either the room was wrong or the location was. It felt more like a Hollywood set than anything real.

Cool air blown in by the air-conditioner silently kept the room at a pleasant temperature to the stifling heat that looked to be outside. The five men seemed ignorant of all this as they either stared at each other, or down at the small display screens recessed into the stone.

There seemed to be no visible hierarchy at first glance, each man wore immaculately tailored suits and black polished leather shoes that would happily grace any boardroom in the world, but which added to the odd vista.

However one man dominated, and held the attention of the other four. He was the oldest of the five by far, his totally bald head deeply wrinkled and peppered with the imperfections of a man in his final years. His thin framed designer glasses, perfectly round, were looking directly into the eyes of the man opposite him. Unlike the other four men, he had no paperwork in front of him, just a small glass crystal tumbler with a finger of amber liquid. His wrinkled finger pressing down on its rim as if preventing it from rising.

“What further information do we have on the woman Matheus?” He said in English, with a strange German accent, and with a commanding voice used to being obeyed.

The man he directed the question to was younger, in his fifties, fitter, with a full head of black hair and an immaculately trimmed beard. The broken nose and the large scar across his face from his left eye to his cheek however spoke of a much different upbringing, one outside of any ivy league college or corporate organisation, his hard, and similar Germanic voice reinforced this image.

“Nothing definite father. We initially thought the woman to be from one of the big Swiss auditors, or maybe some special investigator; but the deeper we investigated the more mysterious she became.”

The man looked down at the document he was referencing on the screen.

“We have had very little dealings with the Swiss outside of the banking sector which we use for many of our worldwide operations due to its high degree of privacy. Our contact there had to dig very deep, and what he came up with was not what we expected, and in fact news to everyone in my operations area.”

All four men looked at him in rapt attention.

“It seems the position may be part of a little known government department there, called the negotiator liaison office. It is an offshoot of the judicial system, but one actually outside of direct government control. Its main function seems to be to act as a neutral third party regarding important decisions or negotiations affecting Swiss interests, such as the one that uncovered our man in the Munich bank acquisition. They also seem to play a significant role in diplomatic and legal matters which my contact seems to think may offer them larger powers still.”

“There was another man arrested at the same time as ours, our guy Mendez knew he wasn't legitimate, asking too many questions, poking his nose in areas that did not belong. He was feeding him false information just in case he was investigating us, it turns out he was, but he was not local law enforcement, he was CIA.”

Still nobody interrupted, but the old man’s eyes widened with even more interest.

“They must have gotten wind of the fact somehow that the money was going to terrorists, but I don't believe the CIA got wind of anything about The ElderNacht.”

The old man interrupted him. “Then the auditor did us both a dis-service and a service. Hopefully The CIA will assume the money syphoned off was for the terrorists and it should not get back any further.” He stood up and his voice took on an ominous tone as he continued unbroken.

“..However assumptions are for idiots. How much did your man know about the eventual destination of the funds, Matheus?”

“Mendez was the only bright spark in the whole Islamic terrorist cell, he was educated at Eton in England and was converted when his brother was shot by the Israelis while he was planting a bomb. He could have had insight that the eventual destination of the funds was actually to us, and some checks through the banking system would have gotten him to some of our legitimate operations. But it also came to light he was skimming millions off the top for himself, if so he had an exit plan..”

The old man's eyes went cold at the allegation of disloyalty, but let his son continue. “He also looked to us to have already abandoned his original Islamic cause and was more interested in himself at the end. The police have charged him with stealing ninety seven million dollars of stolen money, twenty six million was recovered, sixty two million came to us, the rest he seems to have funnelled away. It is also likely he was going for a bigger slice of the pie this time around as we were not told of the twenty six million. It was likely his swansong before he vanished into whatever country he had chosen to hide.”

“I want him dead.” Said the old man seriously. “You need to send a message to your people that we are not to be messed with, also if he does know something he might make a deal, we dare not have the CIA, or any other agencies realise there is a bigger picture.”

“Father.” A weasel-faced man to his right interrupted him, with a raised finger and a crisp American accent. “If I may interrupt for a second. I believe our London cell may have come across this Swiss agency before. It never registered at the time, but I had a man positioned to take over the head of a large credit union that was rejected at the last minute. He had basically passed through every hurdle, but just prior to taking up the position his appointment was blocked for no reason we could come up with at the time, but after digging he was later told that a large Swiss interest had vetoed it.”

“And you think it is the same Swiss auditor involved in both Edmund?” Said the old man, deeply interested.

“The modus operandi was the same father, but the woman was older. He remembers a meeting with an immaculately presented older woman, who asked a few irrelevant questions that made no sense. I also do not believe in coincidences!” Replied the man crisply.

The old man seemed to think deeply for a short while. The others sensed he was about to speak and kept quiet.

“So why have we never heard of this Swiss agency before?” He said to the room, specifically looking at the two men who had not spoken yet. “We are on the cusp of something momentous here, the realisation of our fathers dreams, this is not a time for surprises. I am open to any of your thoughts on this matter.”

“Like Matheus said, father,” a third man responded, “my Asian operations have very little dealings with the Swiss other than to act as our bankers for some of our areas of interest..”

“Do we really need to care, group-leader?” The final, youngest man interrupted. “The Munich operation was a smokescreen, a diversion. With the Artefact and the new information now in the hands of our engineers and scientists, it is only a matter of time, then no country or organisation will be able to stop our goal or dare stand in our way.”

“Ah Ahmed, so official, and hotheaded as always, in this meeting you may acknowledge the fact that I am your father, ‘group-leader’ seems so impersonal when it is just family.”

The old man held his finger up stemming a reply. “Also Ahmed, you must be reading different reports than I am, because from where I am sitting, our science teams have made no real progress yet, and their body count is now well into double-figures.”

The old man turned to the wider group. “It is all a matter of timing. Once the artefact is tamed, then we will no longer care, but until then, if we are discovered, we are still vulnerable to certain state actions. I would rather we proceed on our timescales, not anothers.”

“Then there is another problem father,” the American said seriously, “one we must address sooner rather than later. It seems both CERN and Fermi-lab may be about to join their efforts to locate the anomalous signal. Our source in CERN says the core Fermi-lab team are in negotiations..”

“They have no clue..” Began Ahmed.

“What did I say about assumptions, my son!” Interrupted the old man.

“But father,” Ahmed said, “our teams have made great strides in confirming it is indeed the same artefact lost to the Nazis during the war. The historian is excited about the new books we got from the terrorists, and It looks to be exactly what it says in the scriptures. We are much better placed to deal with this than either our forefathers or the Nazis were.”

“No Ahmed, we are not better placed until we understand it. Remember everybody in that castle died, dozens of people here are dead. We have also not confirmed it is the same artefact, the notes from the castle only talk of the head of the dragon, ours is much more complete. Until we know for certain we only move when either our scientists, the historians, or the keeper of the scrolls find a way to control it, not before.” The old man stared at the other four men.

“Is that understood?”

Everyone said yes.

The old man turned to the man to his right.

“Matheus, have your contact get me a bit more information on this auditor, I need to be sure that Mendez did not leave any trails open for either the auditor or the CIA to discover.”

Matheus nodded curtly.

“Herman, Matheus, Just in case, I want your teams to begin planning the operation to disrupt the CERN and Fermi-lab operations, also if they are indeed going to be working together, then there is an opportunity we can take them out in one go.”

“Yes father,” said Matheus.

“Yes father,” said the sickly looking thin man to his left, in a thin weak voice, his cold unreflecting eyes staring at his father.

The old man reclined back in his seat, turning to the group. “Good. And Ahmed, ensure the lead scientist and the lead historian are at our next meeting. I want to know first hand the progress on the artefact, and whether both men are still suitable to continue in their roles.”

* * *

The New CIA Director sitting at the head of the large table did not look happy. “So Hegan, can you let me know why the German ambassador was called to bail out one of your CIA agents from the German police last week?”

“I will let my operations director answer that sir.” Said the unhappy looking grey haired man two down from the head of the table. He looked across the table to a pasty-faced older woman in a worn tweed suit.

Chambers shifted uncomfortably in her chair. Of course she couldn't tell them! These guys sat in their fancy offices and had never had to make a real operational decision. Sometimes it worked out, sometimes, as in this case, it didn't.

“Unknown sir.” Chambers said in her most professional voice. “The background checks for our operative were done months ago by the bank's HR department. He had been well integrated in the bank's organisation, without any red flags, and was getting active intel right until the time of his arrest..”

Chambers official title was director of delta operations, but it was director in name only, she was middle-management at best, reporting to CIA deputy director Hegan, one of a dozen deputy directors, who all reported to the actual CIA director. It was also just her luck that the CIA director was totally green, having only been appointed two months earlier, and knowing next to nothing of the secret service, or its methods.

Chambers in contrast had come up from the ranks. An average, by the book agent, she was now in her early fifties, and this was likely as high up she was ever going to get. She had been enjoying the power play of running a secret ops department, working at the coal face, but that was until today. It was her first time to meet the new director, and of course it would be under the worst circumstances possible.

Her own manager, deputy director Hegan, was useless, cold, and as usual just trying to distance himself from any responsibility, less the repercussions fell on him. He had been copied on all the mission goals, knew exactly what was happening, yet had provided no support for her or her team in the meeting. It was maddening.

“But your agent was outed by a single auditor,” interrupted another guy, one she did not know, and who nobody had introduced. He continued without a pause, “a single auditor who had by all accounts only been on site for a few days, the same auditor that outed the two people you were looking for, and uncovered that these were the two diverting the funds to the terrorists.”

She knew someone would rub that part in her face. She was livid that a single woman, a girl no less, had taken a couple of days to do what her whole department had not achieved in four months. The auditor woman had a lot more insight than the CIA, which meant she probably had some high powered resources backing her up.

“We only have what we got from the debrief of our agent Sir. The auditor also had the full cooperation of the bank and had full access to everything, something we did not..”

She was interrupted again, by her own boss Hegan, who was talking to the Director. “The German police have released Agent Tanner under house arrest while his status is sorted out, and they have finished interviewing him..”

The director grumbled, looking at both Chambers and Hegan.

“The US government is having to eat a lot of humble pie from the Germans for this. You Chambers got caught running an illegal operation on their territory and falsifying documents, you Hegan failed to supervise your department properly.”

He paused, looking around the room, before settling back on Hegan. “At least they have dropped the charges of our agent being complicit with the thieves, though some of the evidence on your man is pretty damning that he either was on the take or totally naive.”

The director looked back at Chambers. “Did this agent at least get anything from the ringleader,” he consulted his document, “..Mendez?”

“..No sir.” She said timidly.

“Do we know if this Swiss auditor, the..” Again he consulted his notes, “negotiator. Do we know if this negotiator got any more information from Mendez?”

“Er no sir. We have hit a bit of a dead end, the information we have from our agent Tanner is that the negotiator stopped as soon as the crimes were uncovered. She then passed everything to the police, and simply left. It is possible she may have uncovered more that she or the police are not telling us..”

“What the hell even is a negotiator?” The director interrupted, exacerbated and loudly, turning to everyone in the room and seeing a sea of confused faces.

“Anyone?” He turned specifically to Hegan with a hard stare.

“Er, we don't know exactly sir,” Hegan replied, losing a little of his professional demeanour. “I asked the same question, but there is little public information on the organisation she works for and this woman has never been a person of interest to us. Our Information so far is that it is some branch of the Swiss judicial system, though why it got involved in a German bank acquisition is unknown.”

The Director rubbed his temples, keeping his attention on the deputy director. “So Hegan, how does the USA salvage anything from this shit-show your delta operations manager has signed us up to?”

Chambers hated it when the hierarchy, in their ivory towers, spoke in their third-person voice, almost ignoring her presence. They were happy to take all the credit when things went right, but equally happy to shovel all the shit down on those below them when things went wrong.

Hegan stayed quiet, thinking, but the Director interrupted him.

“I will tell you how, I have assigned agent Ethan Curran who is the FBI Interpol chief liaison officer and special investigator. He will do a discreet investigation of the Munich operation, fix up your mess, see what went wrong and see how this negotiator was able to get so far ahead of us.”

“But sir, if I may,” said another man from the centre of the room. “The CIA cleans up its own mess, involving another department may undermine..”

“Thank you Moran, if I need help making decisions I will ask you. Curran is highly recommended and cleared for top secret due to previous work he has done for other agencies. Also he speaks German, Italian and French, he also plays by the rules so there should not be any further screw ups!” He looked at the crowd and especially Hegan and Chambers.

“Is that OK with everyone?”

The question was rhetorical and nobody dared answer it, choosing instead to all keep quiet. Damn, thought Chambers. She had assumed she would be cleaning up her own mess. Involving this agent Curran was unexpected and might cause some issues. She tried to look neutral, her worry however was real.

Hegan shifted uneasily in his chair, ignoring Chambers, and focussing on the director. “Sir I have given orders for Tanner to be brought here and give us all a full debrief as soon as he returns from Munich.”

“Let’s hope he does return, Hegan, and doesn’t skip town. When he gets back I want the corrupt little shit properly questioned, then kicked out to somewhere where he has no responsibility.”

He looked to be finished, then spoke out again. “Oh and Curran better get full assistance from all of you. Remember he is working for me.”

The Director dismissed Chambers and the other middle-managers off-handedly, but kept her boss, Hegan, and another guy for a further hour. Without further information they all finally agreed to do nothing until the full debrief of agent Tanner and the report by agent Curran.

Nobody was happy.

* * *

Michael sat in his temporary hotel room located in the CERN facility, he had almost everything he needed in his one backpack and suitcase. The room was a mess and he felt the usual tinge of nerves about the start of the job tomorrow; Apprehension as he was going to be surrounded by the best of the best and he might not live up to everybody's expectations, and excitement to get started and maybe become part of history with some new insight into the universe.

He arrived at reception on Monday morning and was greeted by one of the HR staff, a young attractive woman, whose looks immediately reminded him of Charlie. She however had none of the sparkle or humour, and every attempt at conversation in French was met by a reply in English. By the fourth attempt at speaking French he gave up and just used English, as it was fairly obvious she thought he was hitting on her.

Michael sank into a quiet stupor as he was shown the main points of the facility, given the welcome documents and speech and finally dumped back at the hotel.

He sighed and continued his unpacking. He had this room for up to four weeks, while he was assigned more permanent accommodation or found his own.

Michael's French immersion had been baptism by fire, but in meeting Charlie, he had been given a lot of incentive to learn, so he pushed himself hard and made himself love every minute. By the final week his personal tutor had mostly stopped using English and had begun taking him around the town, putting him in various situations with different people, in cafes, libraries, and shops, some were awkward, none were easy. The tutor was patient but very relentless and even up to the last day for homework she was making him watch French videos, movies and plays. It was looking to be a long road, but from the confusing mist of befuddlement, pockets of clarity began to arise.

In the hotel room now, alone, it felt like all the work he had put into his cultural integration had been for nothing. He pushed himself out of his stupor and went for a little walk through the shopping area, the gardens, the library and the restaurants.

Everyone he said hello to was polite but he could not get anyone to engage more than a couple of sentences, without feeling weird. He decided to have dinner in the restaurant on the French side of the facility, he saw others sitting alone, little pockets of silence and solitude amongst the spirited conversations going on around them.

He returned to his room a bit depressed, to read the pile of welcome documents the HR woman had given him, and pen a few emails. He had never felt so alone. “I, a universe of atoms, an atom in the universe.” He remembered seeing the Richard Feynman quote in big letters inside one of the school buildings as he passed quickly through it this morning, and it was exactly how he felt before he fell asleep.

Day two was an improvement. He was put in a small group with five other men and one woman, all of them from different countries. It was the proper start of his orientation, and a more thorough introduction to the site and facilities. The process was slow and seemed to dwell on notable achievements and people, most of which he already knew from his own research; But at least he now had other people to talk to, albeit in English, as none were French, and some had not even made the effort to learn even the basics of the local language.

The small group had lunch and a welcome dinner, and finally he began to relax as he found his fellow newbies to be in the same boat as him; Really nice people who had the same feelings and nervousness. By the second day most of the new intake were on first name terms, laughing and joking, and talking about their various experiences and research.

The orientation took so long because the site was extensive, it had nearly three thousand people working there and nearly seventy nationalities represented. Charlie was right, just about everyone spoke English in mixed groups, which was just about every group he saw. He soon realised with over thirty different languages represented it was the only real way anything could be coordinated.

Contrary to popular belief, most of the people at CERN were not scientists. In fact there were only a few hundred proper scientists, and most of the staff were graduates, researchers, technicians, engineers and people who just kept everything running. Their job’s covered everything from designing the massively complex detectors, to the guys running the hotels, cafes and the visitor centres. The whole facility was strangely neither considered part of France nor Switzerland, and you crossed the country boundaries without even being aware of it several times a day.

His group got together that Friday night to eat dinner together. After today, many of them, including Michael, would disappear off into their various departments and areas of research. Michael’s speciality was large data sets, and CERN produced trillions of bytes of data every hour, every byte of which needed to be filtered and sifted through to ensure nothing important was missed.

* * *

Michael was ushered at the start of the second week into the operation managers office, an impressive office in the administration building, an office with its own meeting area and big presentation screen. Another man, older with greying thin hair, brown wrinkled skin, in a white lab coat was already seated, but got up when he arrived.

“Mr James, may I introduce you to your colleague and head of your new department. Professor Bonito.”

Michael shook hands with the man, keeping his grip weak in anticipation of breaking the guy's fingers, the handshake however was surprisingly strong, showing how wrong his first impression was.

“Doctor James, my pleasure,” the man said in accented English, in what Michael considered was a Spanish accent.”

“Just Michael please,” said Michael.

“Then just Carlos for me then,” said the man, indicating a chair, and waiting for Michael to get comfortable.

“Now before you get too excited, Michael, there is little in the way of fame and fortune in this position I’m offering, in fact most scientists I know are myopic in their pursuit of a single goal and wouldn't touch it. We are not talking about exciting physics, just cleaning up and analysing the data we have and are getting, so the real science can be done unhindered.”

Michael was expecting exactly that kind of job anyway, and it was the kind of work he liked so remained neutral despite the initial down-sell.

“Separating the data from the noise is probably the most important task that goes on here, and in a way you will be helping all the other teams get to the bottom of their problems when you do it.”

I would have led with that, thought Michael, still remaining neutral.

“My team is small, but proportionally we have been given a lot of resources and obviously have masses of computing power at our disposal. It was your paper collating world wide power fluctuations due to solar activity, and how you analysed so many different types of data and sorted through the noise that was the main reason we wanted you.”

“You will be given a lot of autonomy in how you do the work, but we also expect you to work as a team with us. Of course we also get called into other projects from time to time to assist in specific data analysis and two of our previous employees have been added to the project teams for major discoveries for the work they did to help. Of course, there is always the possibility of another paper on how we analysed and sorted through the mess.”

Carlos stopped, and seemed to be expecting an answer, as did the other guy who he presumed was either the operations manager or a HR guy.

“I think you have my interest,” said Michael.

* * *

Michael was led by Carlos to a small white Fiat, one of the many similar vehicles he had seen in his travels in the complex. They drove the very short distance, almost at walking speed, to an older dire-looking building, three-stories high, windowless and battleship grey.

Both men got out of the vehicle, Carlos leaving the doors unlocked and the keys in the ignition. There were few people walking around, and the building they were looking at was quite ugly. It wouldn’t be out of place in an industrial estate in any corner of the world.

“This is one of the original buildings, it contains one of the newer data centres, and it also houses another team of scientists called the WOW team. They are a bit secretive, and primarily focussed on finding another anomaly. This building has some of the fastest network connections due to the data centre and we have the downstairs lab where the fibre routers enter the building, making it ideal for our work. We share the space with one of the WOW team’s three detectors and some of the technicians looking after it all.”

It all looked a bit grim to Michael. Was he really going to accept a job in some dingy basement with a small group of guys, not even working on any of the sexy stuff? He was not overly impressed so far, but not really too worried. CERN was his preferred option, but he had already been offered two other jobs in California for double the money so he was under no illusion this was a dead end for him. Even if he hated the job, a couple of years in CERN would look great on his CV.

The first surprise was the door they went through was just a short corridor with large elevator doors at the end. The corridor was well lit with light walls and nice pictures of a few, he supposed, famous scientists on the wall. The sign above the elevator had Einstein's famous quote ‘God Does Not Play Dice’ written in some sort of comic font, Michael smiled, these might be his people after all.

Carlos pressed the elevator call button and the doors opened immediately showing a very deep and high goods elevator which said capacity thirty and three Tonnes.

“Here is where we need the special key,” said Carlos, waving the badge around his neck in front of Michael then holding it up to the tag reader. There were no buttons except for the red one up at eye level which said, ‘Emergency’. The pad turned green and the doors started to close.

“They used to hold the tape backups down here, now the archives are on the big disk arrays and are in the servers up on the ground floor with the WOW team.”

‘Downstairs’ was a bit of a misnomer, the lift went down without stopping for about 30 seconds, there was no progress or floor indicator, and after a slight deceleration the doors opened to a massive well lit open area. Due to its size, the room felt more like a warehouse. Soft jazz music played from a speaker about ten metres up that was tied to some support beam. Michael looked around, awed, it certainly was no dingy basement!

“I saw your face up top, Everybody has the same reaction, even me when I first arrived, it really is quite a nice place to work though,” said Carlos smugly.

“This floor was built for housing a big detector, but the project goals changed a year into the excavation and it was deemed too small and repurposed as the backup tape storage area. It was going to be re-used at a later date but was considered not ideal so we made it our home base. Welcome to the Ghost Lab.”

Michael was impressed, it looked like it would take him months to work through all the equipment and computers in here. As if reading his mind Carlos said, “it is actually a lot simpler than it looks, the majority of that area over there is one of the original particle chambers the WOW team moved here, they replaced the older sensors with more modern sensitive detectors and it is one of the three detectors they use.”

“Why is it called the Ghost lab?” Michael enquired.

“Historical. The room used to hold a small neutrino detector, those are called the ghost particles because of the fact they are everywhere but almost impossible to detect. Even now billions are going right through us, and indeed the whole planet, but with almost no mass they hardly ever interact with any other particle.”

Michael was aware of what neutrinos were, and how we still had little to no idea about them, but was content for Carlos to elaborate.

“Anyway, over there.” Carlos pointed at a long row of flashing lights, “is our computer and temporary storage area, all recently upgraded. There is also a permanent presence of engineers and technicians who work twenty-four hour shifts. These guys are not really anything to do with us, but we share the same area and we are all pretty friendly.”

“Our team is small, just four day staff, but for the past five weeks it has only been three of us. Thomas, our data scientist, developed a long term illness and returned to Portugal, but you make the team back up to four again. Hours are flexible, most of us don't really have a life and tend to work long hours, though technically we are only contracted and paid for thirty-seven. I also don't mind you doing side projects outside your core hours, after all there need to be some perks. Now let's meet the other two guys.”

* * *

He was taken to an area of the room between the computers and the detector where a dozen long benches sat. In between were racks of what look like customised electronics, oscilloscopes, meters and LED panels. Monitor screens filled every spare gap. The room had a large cleared area with five long, wide desks on them, each desk had a workstation with three large monitors each. Two other people sat typing, lost in something and unawares of Carlos and Michael. Carlos coughed loudly and both swivelled their chairs in his direction.

“Anne-Marie, Luc, let me introduce you to Doctor Michael James, the rock star data analyst guy I was telling you about last week.”

He pointed at the smaller person, a woman, “Anne-Marie Verne, Lyon University PhD in Mathematics, specialising in chaos theory.”

Michael stepped up to her and held out his hand. “Enchanted to meet you Miss Verne, please call me Michael,” he said in his best French.

The small woman, in her white lab coat, hiding behind a wall of monitors at first looked a little frightened, like a rabbit caught in a car’s headlight. But looking at the other two men, then back at Michael it changed to a mild look of shyness.

She seemed average in every way, her black hair bunched up on her white face and Michael smiled his best smile until his eyes finally caught hers. They were blue and scared looking, but stayed that way for only a second and then he caught a tiny smile on her face.

“Such a nice accent Michael,” she said demurely in French accented English and weakly shook his hand. “Your French is actually quite good, but we consider it polite to speak in English when we are in mixed company, we reserve French for when everyone listening can speak it, or we are outside the facility; Same goes for all languages, though there are exceptions if you are sure everyone is fluent. You will learn there are a lot of little rules like that around here Michael.”

Carlos stared strangely at Anne-Marie, then at the other man, sharing a momentary look of confusion between them. He then shook his head, stepped forward and pointed at the other man. “And this is Luc Johansson, from Stockholm, a man who seems to defy classification, he is just good at everything.”

Luc smiled and shook Michael's hand with a firm grip, though accompanied with a very big smile. “Just call me Luc, I’m a physicist, who went into chemistry, then into electronics, then computers. Carlos grabbed me before anyone else as I accidentally confessed to also knowing SQL.”

Michael laughed, structured query language was the normal way you analysed large data sets.

“So everything is at least in databases then?” He asked.

“Well, no.” Replied Carlos, “unfortunately some older data sets have not yet moved over, this is where Anne-Marie shines, she designs the digital signal processor software and mathematical algorithms that remove most of the noise and allows us to insert clean, less chaotic, data into the databases.”

Anne-Marie continued to watch Michael curiously, ignoring Luc’s complement. Carlos and Luc were still not quite getting over the fact that she had said more than two words, and had actually kept eye contact with the new man.

“One of our biggest problems unfortunately,” said Luc, shaking his head away from Anne-Marie, “is we have data from the last fifty years, in all sorts of media and formats going all the way back to tape reels. We actually have several old IBM tape readers from the 1970’s and a lovely old data analyst called Nora, who works three days a week in another building, and whose only job for the past twenty five years has been to mount the tapes so our programs can process them..”

“Somebody please kill me if I ever get given that job,” he added to a short smile from everyone.

Michael looked at Luc, he was tall and well built and probably could have played rugby or been a linebacker if he was American, his blonde hair was short and his skin a little pasty, his looks were not helped by this. But at least he seemed fun to work with.

“Why don't we show Michael where the coffee machine is.” Said Carlos, who started to walk away to a corner of the room, everyone followed, Anne-Marie a little behind, and quiet.

“So here is the coffee machine,” said Carlos, pointing out the obvious. “Hot water for tea, there is a fridge with cream and milk, and where you can keep your lunch if you don’t want to go to the cafeteria, just remember the fridge is emptied by the cleaner, Julianne on Monday and Thursday morning, she also stocks the fridge, tea and coffee beans so be nice to her.

“A well run ship!” Commented Michael looking around. His eyes fell on a fish tank with sand in it but no water, Carlos caught his gaze.

“That is Intsywintsy,” said Carlos, “Jez’s pet jumping spiders, she collects them. They are cute but only last a year so don't get too attached.”

Michael looked at a few of the little hairy dots through the glass, he was definitely not going to get attached. Spiders of any sort were not his thing.

“Who is Jez?”

“Jessabelle is one of the technicians I told you about,” said Carlos, “you will get to know them all eventually, they sometimes assist us if we need something done, especially if you feed them chocolate.”

“What’s your poison Michael?” Said Luc.

“Well tea usually,” then he remembered he was not in England, “that’s English breakfast tea, but I am also partial to cappuccino.”

Carlos looked through the colourful collection of tea boxes, ironically not finding anything made from a real tea plant. “Peppermint, chamomile, er, lemon and ginger..”

“Don't worry,” Michael said, “I saw some in the supermarket on site, they must have a lot of English people who miss home, also I suspect my mother will be sending me red-cross parcels of English food, she is very old fashioned.”

Anne-Marie’s curious look broke into a small smirk, she was always a bit apprehensive with new people, but Michael seemed familiar somehow. It was Deja-vu when their eyes first met, almost like someone she had talked to before, or wanted to talk to; It was like that nice boy at school that everybody liked, who always smiled at you when they passed and you were always going to say hi to. And why did she feel so happy?

They all settled down on a couple of old sofas and an armchair to drink. An Asian-looking guy, David, who was actually from Denmark introduced himself, he was also getting two coffees. He and the duty engineer Alfredo, were in the middle of doing a calibration on the B3 array, whatever that was.

“The music is mine,” said Carlos, “my turn today. The playlist is on strict rotation, and if you are not here for your turn, you miss it. No heavy stuff or rap allowed though, my lab, my rules.” He said it jovially. Michael would have to take some of the heavier pieces out of his playlist.

“I’m keen to get started right away,” said Michael, "I have been in Montreux on a French immersion course for almost a month so I need to get back into my work before I get too rusty.”

“Somebody should have told you, you can get by with just English here.” Smiled Anne-Marie. “It is probably the only place in mainland Europe where you can live your whole life without knowing the native language.”

He turned to her and his smile returned. “Well, I wanted to fit in, and being English I only know the one language, so I assumed fitting in would mean speaking French. It's strange, the day before my class started I met this woman who said exactly the same thing you just said, I assumed she was just making conversation.”

“Well I for one am glad you made the effort,” said Anne-Marie, “you will have a lot more choice and get far better service if you can speak French.”

Carlo and Luc stared again at Anne-Marie, then at each other. Michael noticed their interaction, but had no idea why. They finished up their drinks and left the cups rinsed on the sink for later, then Carlos dragged Michael on a tour of the lab. The data centre was amazing, there was a lot of horsepower in the computer systems and they were just scratching the surface, he really could make a difference here.

Carlos had left him a lot to think about that first day. By the time they had finished it had slipped past six o’clock, Carlos apologised for keeping him past the normal leaving time, watched him leave then, hypocritically, went back to his computer.

* * *

Michael really had not much else to do. If he followed the word of the law on the hours he was supposed to work, he would be sat in his tiny bedroom, and bored for most of the week. So with his new access card in hand, he walked into the lab early.

When Michael got to the kitchen with his new unopened box of tea bags, the cleaner was already there emptying the dishes. She wore a simple uniform with a black apron over it, and was rocking her head, humming a tune with some black ear-pods in. That was until Michael surprised her.

“Oh, hi, sorry for startling you, I’m Michael, I’m the new guy here, you must be the cleaner?”

“So, sorry, I don't speak English well,” she stuttered embarrassed.

This was perfect for Michael, he repeated the sentence again in French, to a kind smile, then proceeded to let her know a bit about him and get a little information about her. Her name was Julianne, she had worked here five mornings a week for the last three years. She said she lived in Divonne-Les-Bains, a small village a few kilometres away on the French side of the border, with her husband and two young children.

She was actually quite chatty and, though he didn’t understand every word, her French was generally very clear. Julianne in turn ignored most of his mistakes, he suspected she was just being nice but he really appreciated her for bearing with him.

He heard someone walking up behind him and Julianne looked over Michael's right shoulder looking a little surprised. He turned and saw a new woman, no wait, was that Anne-Marie?

“Julianne, you should leave the poor Englishman alone,” teased Anne-Marie who walked up to her and kissed her softly on each cheek, then turned to Michael and did the same.

Anne-Marie had spent hours last night telling herself she could do it, had to do it. Her dreams were full of the new man, they were both laughing and he was smiling and holding her hand. The dreams were strange, but they were also beautiful. If Michael and Julianne had known the mental exercises that Anne-Marie had gone through in preparation for this small moment they would have been shocked. To her relief, both accepted the normal salutation with a smile.

Julianne hid her slight feeling of shock at the salutation. The first time she met Anne-Marie she gave a similar salutation and turned the poor girl into an almost quivering wreck. She looked at her blue eyes, now trained exclusively on the new man.

“Shame on you Michael, breaking the first rule, and talking French when Julianne needs to improve her English.”

Michael continued to smile, Anne-Marie had said everything so far in French, and something was definitely a little different about her today. Her medium black hair, that he did not notice yesterday, was pristine, her face had a little more colour, and her lab coat was open with a nice top under it and some attractive, if a little white, legs below her knee-length skirt. He didn’t really think anything of it yesterday, but he now thought she was probably quite pretty, and wondered what she looked like outside of the lab environment.

Julianne, was still getting over the shock from the change in Anne-Marie’s appearance and countenance, and after a positive, encouraging smile directed at Anne-Marie, she went back to her chores a little confused, leaving them both talking, but as usual listening in.

As they talked, Anne-Marie found she did not struggle for words, and had none of her usual anxiety-attacks, for some reason she even carefully corrected some of his bigger grammatical mistakes.

The lift opened and Carlos and Luc walked in and Anne-Marie switched to English mid-sentence.

“..I think it is Luc’s turn today to go over his work and take you through the formatting of the datasets,” she finished.

“Thank you Anne-Marie,” Michael said, genuinely happy, “I really wish we could continue our conversation in French, there is so much I want to talk about and I find you so easy to talk with, it is unfair we cannot do it at work.”

He smiled a little too teasingly before he walked over to Luc, and the only thing she thought about for the rest of the day was that smile and how happy she was feeling.

“What the hell have you unleashed!” Luc said, shaking his head, as they got out of Anne-Marie's earshot. “She looks totally different, and has never opened up this much to anybody. I think she must either really like you a lot, or I am going to have a word with security to see if she has been replaced by some alien lizard-double or a pod-person.” Michael laughed, getting the bad sci-fi references.

“Anyway, today you are all mine and we have a lot to do, let's sort your workstation and accounts up so you can get started.”

Michael was thrown deep into the massive system architectures of the various projects, but grasped some of the biggest problems they were having almost immediately. The set of programs he had written for his dissertation would probably be perfect, but he bet they knew that already. Everytime he caught a brief glimpse with Anne-Marie, they both smiled, he would most definitely be making the effort to get in early.

# Deeper and Darker

“HI MARLINE, WHAT IS it?” Said Charlie to the young woman who had walked up with a small blue bucket. Marline was a shy, unpretentious girl, honest to a fault, hard working, but little in the way of ambition. Charlie thought she would be quite a prize catch for the right man if she ever got out of her shell and met one. She was one of two girls in the large office cleaning teams that Charlie and Gerty had found trustworthy, and the only two allowed access to these offices.

Charlie of course already knew what Marline wanted, the question had formed in the girl’s mind as she approached.

“Well miss, er, can you come to the window, please.” Charlie never questioned her, but got up and walked with her to the window. The girl had seen some weird box attached to the glass behind the drapes and was wondering if she should clean there.

Marline moved the drapes aside and she and Charlie looked at the box. Charlie moved her finger in front of her lips in the universal gesture not to talk. Then walked her out of the office into the corridor.

“Thank you Marline, can you leave the box for now, and please don't mention it to anyone.”

“No miss, not a word.” She said as they both walked back into the office.

She caught the thought that Marline would indeed never mention it to anyone. The big bonus that she and the other trusted cleaner earned for this one office to keep anything they heard or saw a secret, was a pinky-promise she would never break.

Charlie was in the office to debrief Alice, who was their normal liaison to the various government departments or clients. Alice would write the reports, field calls, sort out expenses and issue any invoices if necessary. She was gold.

The offices had of course been bugged before. Because Charlie and Gerty routinely dealt with big corporations, governments and police departments, they had had several incidents in the past, and had procedures in place with the government security services, the FIS, to fix.

She grabbed Alice as she walked through the door and turned her around to walk back to the corridor, once outside and the doors had closed, Charlie whispered smiling.

“The office is bugged again!”

Alice smiled. “Life never gets boring around here! The security guys were only here two weeks ago so it must have been done recently. Shall I call the exterminators?”

“Better had, though we should probably find out who is doing it and for what reason.”

Alice’s history was almost as interesting as Gerty's. She left the military at the age of twenty two to take up a position in the FIS, She was always seen as an efficient administrator and analyst and eventually became the PA to the director there, moving to the Swiss legal system where she ended up as Gerty’s assistant. She followed Gerty as she became the negotiator.

Alice shook her head, “I have no clue how you are going to do that and I am not going to ask.”

When they got back, Alice took some large signs out of a cupboard and hung them on the walls. They said in big letters ‘The office is bugged!’.

* * *

During the belated debrief with Gerty the next day over breakfast, Charlie speculated as to who was bugging them. They looked at the most recent cases. The one in Ankara was purely a negotiation gig, to ensure both parties remained honest, five suppliers and six people were casually excluded from the negotiations due to private agendas but these guys were executives and middle managers, not secret agents, Charlie promised to review her memories to see if she missed anything.

Gerty's recent case at the Interpol offices in Nice could also be dismissed, apart from being present for the interviews and asking some pertinent questions she was never publicly acknowledged to even be there.

“What about the Munich case?” Said her mother.

“Yes, that's the one I was thinking of too. It was unusual in that one of the guys I handed to the police, Tanner, was working unbeknownst to us as an American agent of some type. I knew he was an agent, and that he was effectively looking into the same thing I was, but he was also quite dirty, and defrauding the bank for his own financial gain. I thought I would just cut the interview, collate the evidence and let the police sort out the mess.”

Gerty nodded. “There was a bit of a stink about it in the German press, it does seem to have soured the relationships between the two countries a little.”

“My other take,” continued Charlie, unfazed by the fact she had directly contributed to the relationship-souring between the two nations, “was that the money syphoned off by the actual main culprit, Mendez, looked to be going to support terrorist cells, which explained the CIA’s interest. But he also had other intentions which I didn't feel at the time that I had to dig any further into..”

Charlie paused thinking. “You know Mama, I got to be friends with the senior commander of the police force there, the arrests looked good for his department, and he did actually say if I needed anything I only had to ask.”

“I assume you are going back to Munich to call in that favour then?” Smiled Gerty.

“I think so Mama.”

“Well just be careful darling, I know you have Carl with you, and can take care of yourself, but none of these guys are nice people.”

Both women relaxed, going back to the usual less serious mother and daughter chatter. It was a few minutes later when Charlie was taking a sip of her coffee when she looked at her mother with curious eyes.

Gerty met her stare with one of her own. “No Charlie, I did not set you up with an English scientist in Montreux!” She said, shocked.

“But as your mother I feel you should tell me more..”

* * *

Rail was usually her preferred choice for longer journeys, the four and a half hour journey to Liechtenstein suited Charlie so she could catch up on some papers she was reading. Carl as usual hardly spoke. It wasn't that he was anti-social, just quiet and professional.

She had coaxed a little of his story out of him over the time they were together, but he spoke the barest minimum and never initiated any of the conversations, so it usually got boring quickly.
